[trialAccount t18420404-1359]
1359. GEORGE EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 1 purse, value 1 l.; 4 sovereigns, 3 half-crowns, and 3 shillings; the property of Amelia Bowling, from her person.
AMELIA BOWLING. I am a widow, and live at No. 17, Cardington-place, Hampstead-road. On the 31st of March, about nine o'clock, or a quarter past, in the evening, I was passing through Wigmore-street—I felt some one touch my side, and a drag at my dress—I saw the prisoner at my side—I put my hand to my pocket, where I had my purse and some keys—the prisoner had one end of the purse in his hand—I caught hold of the other end of it—he pulled it through my fingers, and got it, then turned and ran away down a mews close by—I pursued him, and saw him taken, without losing sight of him—I had some silver at one end of my purse, and four sovereigns at the other—I heard some money fall on the stones as he turned round again to meet me.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you get your gold again? A. No, I did not pick it up, because I could not see it—I did not see the prisoner's face at the time he took it—his head was down—he was close to me—I pursued him down the mews, and saw him taken—I am quite sure he is the man—Bowling is not my husband's name—I do not like to tell his name—I was christened Amelia Bowling—they are my two Christian names—I have gone by that name for the last twelve months, and took a house in that name—I have been told that my husband is dead, but I
never made any inquiries—it is three or four years since I saw him—I am under the protection of my friends—I am not living with any one to whom I am not married—I live by myself and daughter and servants—I have had lodgers—I have property to live on, money advanced me by a relation—I do not wish to tell any more—I understand my husband died in America about two years ago—my maiden name was Nicholas—a stranger informed me of my husband's death, and said the family were in mourning—I saw none of the family myself—I would rather be excused saying under what circumstances my husband went away—I had much rather he did not return—the person who informed me of his death came from Maid-stone, where the family was residing.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420404-1360]
1360. SARAH JONES and MARGARET PRICE were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 purse, value 1 s.; 3 sovereigns, 1 half-sovereign, and I sixpence; the property of Thomas Stacker, from his person.
THOMAS STOCKER . I am a linendraper, in Charterhouse-lane. On the 6th of April I was passing by Fann-court, Miles-lane, Thames-street, about ten minutes after eleven o'clock at night—I saw both the prisoners together—Jones asked me to treat them to a glass of ale—Price immediately joined her, and said, "Oh do, I am very thirsty"—I made no reply—a friend of mine, who was with me, told them both to go along—I walked along, wished my friend good night, and walked away—Jones then followed me, took hold of my arm, and again asked me to give her a glass of ale—Price kept away—I did not see her then—I walked away towards Thames-street-hill, went to the corner of Thames-street, and called for a glass of porter, which I drank—Jones followed me into the public-house—when I had drank my porter, I called to the person who served me to draw Jones a glass of ale—she did so, I immediately paid for it, and left the house, leaving Jones there drinking her ale—I had not got above a dozen yards from the house before Jones came up to me again—I then turned into Miles-lane, and, on passing Fann-court, Jones pulled me into the court, before I had time to get away—she got her arms round me, and completely pinioned me—she then got her arms round behind me, and it was at that time she took my purse out of my coat-pocket—no one else was in sight at the time—I had taken my purse out not above three minutes before, and taken a sixpence out of it, to pay for the ale and porter—I put my purse into my coat-pocket again after paying for it—I am quite sure of that—when she had taken the purse from my pocket she let go her arms, and I walked away immediately—Price was on the outside of the court, not a yard from the court—I saw her there scarcely a minute after Jones had taken my purse—I saw Jones, with my purse in her hand, endeavouring to pass it to Price—she did not do so—I made a snatch at it, but could not get it—she wheeled round on her heel—they then walked away together—I told Jones she had robbed me of my purse—Price was standing close by at the time—they said it was nothing of the sort, and Price said to Jones, "Caroline, have you got the gentleman's purse?"—Jones said, "No; me have the gentleman's purse! I never thought of such a thing"—I called for the police, and they walked away towards the dry arch of London-bridge—by the time I called for the police again we had arrived at the steps of London-bridge—Jones made a bolt, and got
clear from me—I had hold of her cloak at the time, and was calling for the police—I had not hold of Price—Price immediately laid hold of me—Jones got away from me, and ran up the steps of London-bridge—I got away from Price, and followed Jones—I overtook her near the top of the steps, and gave her in charge—I left her in charge, and told the policema there was another concerned in it—I went after Price, and found her standing outside the court—I laid hold of her, and about that time the other officer came up—I gave her in charge—I suspected they had dropped the purse, and found it in the kennel outside Fann-court, within a foot of where Price was standing—this is it—there was a sixpence left in it—the three sovereigns and a half were taken out.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is your friend here who you say was with you when you addressed these women first? A. He is not—I know where he lives—I could have had him here if there had been any occasion for it—I went into the public-house of my own accord—I have stated that it was at the solicitation of one of the prisoners—neither of them compelled me to go in—I have stated I went in for the purpose of getting rid of her—it was for the purpose of getting rid of her I drank the porter—I drank the porter because I did not like to go in and give a female any thing to drink without drinking myself—I had drank nothing during the whole day but water and coffee.
JOHN CALLAN ( City police-constable, No. 584.) On the night of the 6th of April, I was in King William-street between eleven and twelve o'clock—Jones was given into my custody, and charged by the prosecutor with robbing him of his purse—she said nothing at the time—he said there was another concerned in it below the steps—Price was afterwards given into my brother officer's custody—I saw the prosecutor take up this purse.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was the prosecutor sober? A. Quite so.
MICHAEL DENNIS LAWRENCE ( City police-constable, No. 440.) On the night of the 6th of April, Price was given into my custody at the entrance of Fann-court—I had a Ianthorn with me—the prosecutor picked up this purse about a foot from where Price was standing.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Price did not attempt to go away at all? A. No.
JONES **— GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Ten Years.
PRICE— NOT GUILTY .

1362. HENRY CARTER was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 brass coffin-plate value 5 s., the goods of Samuel Cornel,—5 other COUNTS, stating it to belong to different persons.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
BENJAMIN JOHNSON. I am sexton of the parish of Chelsea, and live
in upper Manor-place, Chelsea. I have the charge of the vaules under the New Church at Chelsea, and keep the keys—there are three public vaults—a person named Hillyer is the constant grave-digger—the prisoner assisted him occasionally—Hillyer was often intrusted with the keys to get matting out, to cover the graves over, when he was going to dig a deep grave—he had only to go to a place just at the entry of the vaults, which was separated by an iron railing from the vaults—on the 23rd of March, there was to be a funeral in one of the public vaults at two o'clock—all the arrangements were made for it—the place had been previously selected where the coffin was to be placed—there was nothing to do, as to looking for the coffins of the relatives or anything of that sort—I gave the key to Johnson, the gate-keeper, that day—he went down to sweep the place up, about an hour or so before the funeral took place—the prisoner had no business in the vault at all—he was not employed by me—there was nothing for him to do there—after the funeral, in consequence of something that transpired a search was made in the vaults, and we missed some handles and plates from the coffins—there was a brass plate gone from Mrs. Lucy Goddard's coffin.
DANIEL JAMES JOHNSON . I am son of the sexton. About one o'clock on the 23rd of March, my father sent me to the vault where the funeral was to take place—I did not find any one in the vault the first time—the second time I went, about half-past one, I found the prisoner there in among the coffins, with a candle out of a candlestick—I asked what business he had there—he said he was looking at the names on the coffins—he did not say what for—the candle then touched something, and went out—I do not know whether it was done by accident or for the purpose—the place was then dark, and I could not see what was going on—I went to fetch a light, and was gone five or ten minutes—he got out, and went to a flue to warm himself—he was not in the vault when I came sack.
JOHN CLARKSON . I am a dealer in marine stores, and live in College-street, Chelsea. On the 23rd of March, the prisoner came to my shop about the middle of the day, and brought 141bs. of lead, and 2lbs. and a half of brass—I paid him 10 d. for the plates—I did not examine it in his presence—when I first saw it had the appearance of brass bent and beat up in a roll, and doubled again—I opened it after he was gone, and found an inscription on it, with the name of "Lucy Goddard"—I should say it was a coffin-plate—I sold it again to Gatfield—I asked him his name and address—I had no conversation with him on that occasion—on Monday, the 28th March, after I had seen a bill, I stated what I had seen to the churchwardens.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You saw the bill offering the reward of 20 l.? A. Yes—I expected the bill implied that the person who gave information was not to be punished—I do not know whether I should have stirred in the matter but for that—as far as I am concerned the reward is but 5 l.—they have not promised me 5 l.—I do not expect to get the whole 20 l.—I sell Gatfield all my brass—he was at my place all that day—I keep the brass by me as long as it suits me to do so, sometimes a week, and sometimes not five minutes—it depends on when I am going to sell it—I did not keep this long—I did not deny to any one that I had received any coffin-plates, before the bill came out—I swear that—no one came to make any inquiries about coffin-plates before the bill came out—
the first application made to me on the subject was by a policeman who came with the bill in his hand—I have kept my shop three yean and a half—I gave two men into custody at Queen-square, on the 1st of March, for bringing me stolen property—I saw no other coffin-plates on the 23rd—I had seen some two days previously—I saw some with the name of Wors. ley, and a coronet on them—I told the churchwardens of that when I told about this, when the bill came out—I had then sold the plates I did not tell any one what I had bought, till after the policeman came to me with the bill, but my place was always open, so that any one might have come and seen what I bought
MR. BODKIN. Q. Who brought the plate with the coronet on it? A The prisoner—he had been there once before—I do not recollect him any more—I keep a book in which I enter my purchases—I entered this—I produced my book at Queen-square—I have not got it here—I have an extract from it—I entered it as so much brass.
THOMAS GATFIELD . I assist Clarkson occasionally. On the 23rd of March I was at his place, and saw the prisoner come there with some brass and lead—Clarkson bought it of him—I forget what the amount was—it was weighed—Clarkson went out, and while he was gone I said to the prisoner, "You have been shaking the dead, have you not," (I had looked at the brass on a former occasion, and saw what it was)—he said, "It is a perquisite I and my mate have, we belong to the churchyard, and we dug it up"—I afterwards saw the plate unfolded, and saw the name of "Lucy Goddard" on it—I sold that with other brass at Mr. Frost's, a large dealer, who buys it for melting.
Cross-examined. Q. How soon after did you take this brass away? A. The same evening—I give Clarkson 5 d. a lb. for all I have of him—he gives 4 d. for it, and I get 6 1/2 d. from the melter—I saw the bill offering 20 l. reward—that did not open my mouth—I shall not get any part of it I dare say—I should not have come about it if the policeman had not come after me—Clarkson sent the police after me—I never bought coffin-plates before these.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Why did you put the question to him about "shaking the dead?" A. I observed that he had brought coffin-plates on former occasions—the plates were broken up when I took them to Frost's—they were only bent when the prisoner brought them, but Clarkson ordered them to be broken up—I took 11 1/2 lb. of brass altogether to Frost's on the 23rd—the 2 1/2 lb. which the prisoner brought was mixed with other metal, and carried away altogether—I have no interest in the reward—if there is anything for me I shall be very thankful.
JAMES JOHNSON . I am a gate-keeper in Chelsea pansh. After this discovery a tool was shown to me—it was the single of the garden line, which I had been in the habit of using in the yard, but it was altered a good deal since I left off using it—it then had a round point, but when it was show to me it had been made flat, like a chisel or screw-driver.
JOHN CAPERER . I am an undertaker. I was employed to conduct the funeral of Lucy Goddard—her coffin was placed in No. 2 vault, under Chelsea church—a brass engraved plate was placed on the coffin, with "Mrs. Lucy Goddard" on it—I have looked at the coffin since this discovery, and the plate is gone, also two more from coffins of the same family.
Cross-examined. Q. Who paid you for the coffin and the funeral expenses?
A. Mrs. Ann Goddard, her sister, since deceased—Mrs. Lucy Goddard was buried in October, 1829, and she died in Sloane-street, Chelsea.
HENRY KIMBER . I am a policeman. I was present when the prisoner was taken—I searched his coat, Which was hanging up in the yard—he said it was his—I found some small fragments of metal at the bottom of the pocket, such as bits of pewter.
— LOVELL. I am solicitor for the prosecution. I have searched at Doctors' Commons through the years 1829, 1830, and 1831, for a will of administration of Mrs. Lucy Goddard, but did not find it—I was present when this matter was examined into before the Magistrate—the prisoner made a statement, which was taken down in writing—I saw him sign it—I did not see the Magistrate sign it.
JAMES SHEPHERD . I am a police-inspector. I saw Mr. Gregorie, the Magistrate, sign this statement—( read)—"The prisoner on examination says, When I went to Mr. Clarkson, he never said any words to me; I do not believe he opened the plates at all; he said if I brought any more he would bay them of me. H. Carter."
JOHN LOVELL . I am in the service of Mr. Frost, a dealer in metal, in Clerkenwell. On the 23rd of March I bought 11 1/2 lbs. of brass of a person named Gatfield—I cannot say whether it was the witness—I remember his being at our shop several times.
Cross-examined. Q. All's fish that corned to the net in your house? A. We do it in the regular way of business—we look at it to see what it is, but when a thing is broken up we cannot see—if anything comes which we think not right, we refuse it.
RICHARD HARVEY GIRAUD . I am a solicitor. Thomas E. Jeffery and Thomas Symonds are the churchwardens of this parish—Mr. Samuel Cornell is clerk to the trustees—Mr. George Bay is one of the trustees of the church, and there are several others.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months. (There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

1364. WILLIAM CARPENTER was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of February, 1 table, value 2 l. 4 s.; 1 bedstead, value 2 l. 18 s.; and 1 chair, value 5 s.; the goods of Joshua James Edmondson and another; to which he pleaded

1365. SAMUEL FRENDGROVE was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 1 shirt, value 3 s. 6 d. the goods of Stephen William Kitchi er; and 1/2 lb. weight of tea, the goods of Hannah Robinson.
MARTHA MARY ANN KITCHINER . I am the wife of Stephen William Kitchiner, and live in New-street, Limehouse—I know the prisoner by his coming to my house to dinner and tea. On the 25th of March, which was Good Friday, he came about half-past ten o'clock in the morning, spent
the day there, and left about ten at night—my husband's shirt was in the house when the prisoner was there—I missed it next morning—this now produced is it—I can swear to it.
Prisoner. Q. Did not Hannah Robinson wash the shirt for me' A. No.
HANNAH ROBINSON . I am the prosecutrix's sister, and am living with her at present. I left my situation on the Monday before Good Friday—I was with her that day—I had some tea in my box, which was left unlocked in the parlour—the hinges were off it—I had bought the tea—I missed it next morning—the prisoner had been left in the room alone several times while he was there—this is my tea.
Prisoner. Q. When you were at your situation did not you ask me to come there several times, and did not I refuse? A. It is false—you never slept all night with me—you are a base young man—you never gave me 5 s.
MARY QAMPBELL . I live in East-road, Limehouse. The prisoner was my lodger—he came home on the 25th of March about half-past ten o'clock at night—he had this shirt in his coat-pocket—he put it on the kitchen dresser, and when he went to bed he took it up with him—next morning I saw this tea in a bundle with the shirt—I said to him, "This is Mr. Kitchiner's shirt"—he threw it away in the street—I picked it up, and afterwards gave it to the policeman—the prisoner paid me 2 s. for his lodging and left—he said nothing—I did not know he was going away—he did go, and my husband followed him—I called the prosecutrix to my house, and showed her the shirt.
Prisoner. Q. When I came home, did not you say, "Is that the shirt that Hannah made for you?" and I said, "Yes?" A. It is false.
ROBERT BYLES . I am a policeman. In consequence of information I apprehended the prisoner at the Ship and Half Moon public-house, at Woolwich—he had this tea in his hat in a hat-box—he said he had bought it with some more things to send down to his mother.
Prisoner's Defence. I have been acquainted with Hannah Robinson about twelve months; she was always asking me to her master's home during his absence, and one Saturday, about three months ago, I stopped with her all night; on the Monday following she said, "I have got a good half-pound of tea to send to your mother, and have bought you two shirts;" I refused taking them; I gave her 5 s., and said that when the finished them I would give her the remainder of the money—about three weeks ago she came to my house and said she was in the family way, that she had told her master, and he had discharged her; she wanted me to marry her, which I was not able to do, but said I would marry her in the course of a month, and she has done this through spite; when I was at
Mrs. Kitchiner's, on Good Friday, she gave me this shirt, and the hand. kerchief to take it home in; when I got home my landlady said, "Have you got the shirt Hannah made for you!" I said, "Yes;" I paid the landlady and went away, leaving the shirt on the bed; I went to Woolwich to get employment, and was taken there; is it feasable I should steal the shirt and tea when I had 22 s. about me at the time? she gave it to me, and said she should not want the money, I should want it myself another time.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420404-1366]

GEORGE COMARFORD . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.

1368. ANN COUTTS was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 2 pillows, value 2 s.; 1 tea-caddy, value 5 s.; 2 pictures, framed and glazed, value 2 s.; and 2 paintings and frames, value 2 s.; the goods of Francis Joseph Green.
FRANCIS JOSEPH GREEN . I am a carpenter, and live in Berwick-street, St. James. On Monday, the 4th of April, the prisoner came and took a furnished room in the name of Coutts, representing herself as a married woman—a/ man came as her husband on the Sunday following—she was there alone on Friday and Saturday, as far as I know—I did not see any one else—I saw the articles stated safe in the room between seven and eight o'clock on the Friday evening, when she came—on the Thursday morning following I looked through the window and missed the carpet—the door was locked, and the prisoner had the key, and also a latch-key of the street-door—I went into the room on the Thursday morning with my wife, and missed the articles—I sent for a policeman, and gave the prisoner in charge—she said she knew nothing about them—she afterwards delivered up three duplicates to the officer, and said she intended to redeem them—the things now produced are mine—I have never seen the caddy or picture-frames since.
JAMES MOODY . I am in the service of James Aldous and Son, pawnbrokers, in Berwick-street. This carpet was pledged at my masters' by the prisoner—the duplicate now produced is what I gave her.
JOHN CARVER . I am shopman to Mr. Ben ton, a pawnbroker, in Tottenham-court-road. One of these pillows was pledged at my master's, (I believe by the prisoner,) for 1 s., in the name of Ann Rogers, lodger, No. 10, Rouse-street—this is the duplicate I gave.
THOMAS MARCHANT . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—I told her what it was for—she gave me these three duplicatcs, one for a carpet, and the others for one pillow each.
Prisoner. I know nothing about the caddy or the pictures.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Six Months.

1369. MARY FENNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 2 beds, value 1 l; 1 blanket, value 6 d.; and 1 clock, value 10 s.; the goods of Bridget Henessey.

WILLIAM SMITH . I am a broker, and live in Turk's-row, Chelsea On Saturday week last I bought this bed of the prisoner for 1 s. 6 d.
✗PHBBE WARE . I am a broker, and live in Turk's-row, Chelsea. I was in my shop last Saturday fortnight—the prisoner came to roe and asked if I would buy a bed—I went with her to Week's-buildings and bought this one for 4 s.—I afterwards gave it up to the policeman.
ROBERT JACKSON . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner, and have produced the two beds, which have been in my care—I have the clock, which I took from one Sarah Bunn—I do not know how she came by it, except from what she herself said—the Magistrate did not bind her over.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. The other woman took two of the beds. I only took the eighteen-penny one. I did not know her. We were in liquor, and I was beside myself.

1370. JOSEPH ISAACS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 jacket, value 10 s.; 1 knife, value 4 d.; 1 chisel, value 3 d.; 1 tobacco-box, value 2 d.; and 2 keys, value 1 s.; the goods of John Davis.
JOHN DAVIS . I am a labourer, and live in Star-street, Paddington. No the morning of the 5th of April I was at work at Hyde-park-corner, open ing a drain—I hung my jacket on the rails by the triumphal arch, near where I was working—I had a tobacco-box, a knife, a chisel, and two keys, in the pocket—I saw the prisoner standing by, just before a coal-wagon came by—I looked round the hind part of the coal-wagon, and saw him running away with my jacket—I ran after him, calling "Stop thief," and he dropped it—I took him, and my fellow-workman picked up the jacket, and gave it to the policeman—this now produced is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You say you saw him with the jacket? A. Yes, under his left arm.

1371. GEORGE NORDBLADE was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 1 copper, value 1 l. 5 s.; and 90lbs. weight of lead, value 7 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Glanville, and fixed to a building; to which he pleaded GUILTY. [trialAccount t18420404-1372]
1372. LOUISA COOKSLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 1 shawl, value 4 s., the goods of Michael Griffith.
MARGARET GRIFFITH . I am the wife of Michael Griffith, of No. 20, George-street, Bloomsbury—we keep a cook-shop. On the 24th of March, between six and seven o'clock in the evening, the prisoner was in our kitchen, having something to eat—I had never seen her before—I left the room for two or three minutes, leaving the prisoner, my son, and Booth by there—on my return the prisoner was gone, and I directly missed my shawl from the cradle, which was there when I had gone ont—I have never seen it again.
GEORGE BOOTHBT . I lodge at Mr. Griffith's. On the evening of the 24th of March I was in the kitchen, and saw the prisoner there—when the prosecutrix went out, she got up, took the shawl out of the cradle, put it on her shoulder, and went out—I did not stop her—I thought it was her own—I had never seen it in Mrs. Griffith's possession before.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX . I am a policeman. The prisoner was given into my charge—she denied having the shawl—no shawl was found on her or in her room.
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.

1374. MARY HUNT was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 half-sovereign, 1 half-crown, 2 shillings, and 1 sixpence; the monies of William Williams, from his person.
WILLIAM WILLIAMS . I am a draper, and live in Vauxhall-street, Lambeth. On the 5th of April I was in Tothill-street, Westminster, going home, and saw the prisoner—I did not know her—she spoke to me first, and took hold of my arm—I desired her to go her way, and leave me to mine—I believe she observed a policeman, and she did leave me for a moment—after I had walked about twenty yards, she came to me again, took my arm, and asked which way I was going—I told her to leave me altogether—she did not, but took hold of my arm, and in a minute gave me a turn round the corner of a street—she directly put her fingers into my right-hand waistcoat pocket, and took out 15 s.—I had changed a soveregin not long before, and had counted my money before I met her—I had it in my waistcoat-pocket not ten minutes before I met her—after she had taken it I felt in my pocket, and missed it—I did not like to accuse her of robbing me, and walked about twenty yards down the street with her, expecting to find a policeman—I then asked her whether she would, or would not, give me up the money that she had taken from me—she directly got from me, and ran away—she gave me chance down two or three turnings—I was close on her the whole time—I did not lose sight of her—I caught her again by Duck-lane—she instantly called out for the assistance of a man or two—she called out " Joe " and a man directly came up with a girl—directly she found the man had come up she threw the money away—the man and girl picked some of it up—the prisoner then wanted to get away from me—I 'still kept hold of her—she then caught my hand
in her mouth, and bit a large piece out of it, and also bit two of my fingers—I kept hold of her till she bit my fingers so severely that I was obliged to loosen her—I then caught hold of a portion of her frock, singing out very loudly for a policeman—one came up, and the man and girl who picked up part of the money made off—I picked up a half-crown and two shillings, which the prisoner had thrown away, close to my feet—I swear positively that the man picked up some.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you see a policeman when the prisoner spoke to you? A. I did, on the opposite side of the way, when she took hold of my arm—I desired her to leave me, but if a female speaks to me I am not going to call for a policeman directly—she did not walk with me arm-in-arm for about thirty yards—she walked arm-in-arm with me from the corner, which was, I suppose, about twenty yards, after she had picked my pocket—it might be between twenty and thirty yards—she took my arm, and walked with me—it was against my will, certainly—I had been to sup with a friend that night, who is a publican—I accidentally dropped in as a friend—I did not pay for my supper—I did not count my money at my friend's house, but in coming along the street—I generally count my money after going anywhere, or before I go to bed, or before I go home—I have, perhaps, counted it going along the street—I counted it in the street that night, to see what money I had in my pocket—I was perfectly sober—I cannot say exactly what money I had in my pocket—I told the Magistrate the prisoner called out for a man or two—she called out for " Joe" and " Tom "—I believe I told the Magistrate that—I swear I mentioned the names of the two—my deposition was read over to me—I was desired to attend to it—I did so, and signed it—I am sure that what I have stated about Tom and Joe was read out to me—( The witness's deposition being read, contained no mention of the prisoner's calling for Tom and Joe)—to the best of my recollection it was read to me—I believe it was—the Magistrate asked me what parties she called out and I told him—I cannot say whether the clerk put it down or not—she did call it out—I cannot swear whether the clerk read it out to me—I stated it, and I supposed it was in my deposition—I took no liberties with the prisoner—my money was not in a purse, but loose in my pocket.
SAMUEL BRINE ( police-constable K 33.) On the evening in question I heard a cry of "Police"—I found the prosecutor holding the prisoner by the arm at the corner of Duck-lane—he gave her in charge, and told me in her presence that she had robbed him of 15$.—I asked him what it consisted of—he said half-a-sovereign, half-a-crown, two shillings, and sixpence—I took the prisoner to the station, and found on her 2 s. 1 d.—the prosecutor's hand was bleeding when I came up to him.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Ten Years.

1381. MARY ANN DUNN was indicated for feloniously taking away, on the 29th of March, a certain male child, aged about three weeks, the son of Joushus and Harriett Dawes.
HARRIET DAWES . I am the wife of Joshua Dawes, a porter, of parker-street, Drury-lane. On Tuesday, the 29th of March, I was in a public-house in seven Dials, with my infant—it was then three weeks and two days old—it was registered— it had not been christened—it was called josephDawes—I saw the prisoner in the public house with a soldier she what a fine baby you have got she asked me to let her hold the child and took it out of my arms I wanted to give it to my husb and who was present he was near enough to prevent its being taken without my will if he chose she replied," I will take it" we were a quarter of an hour there i went away for about ten minutes, upsatirs with my little girl, and when I came back the prisoner was gone I hade left it in care of the prisoner, my husband and the soldier—I found the child again on Saterday, at Croydon—the officer came on Friday morning and told me of it—I saw it at Bow-street—this is the child now in my arms.
Prisoner. You say I took it from you, you brought the child and laid it over the counter, and said "Take the child"—I took it, and you would out. Witness. I asked my husband to take it, and you said you would take it
Prisoner. That was not the first public-house I met you at. Witness. We went from there to the Sugar-loaf, in King-street-my—husband took another person, thinking she was the same woman.
JANE SOPHIA HEYWARD . I am the wife of Eleazar Heyward, a farmer at Croydon. On Tuesday night, the 29th of March, between ten and eleven, I heard a child cry—I went out and saw the prisoner sitting on the shafts of a cart, opposite my house—I asked her what was the matter—she said "Nothing"—I asked her how far she had to go with that infant-she said "To Linfiel"—I asked her if she was going to walk—she said "yes"—I asked her to come in and warm the child—she came in, and said she had been confined in Lambeth workhouse—I asked her how old the child was—She said Three weeks—I asked her why she came out so soon—she said they wanted to take the baby from her, and send her out as
wet nurse—I procured a lodging for her at the Anchor, at Croydon and paid for it.
WILLIAM PRENDERGAST . I received information and went to the An. chor at Croydon, and found the prisoner there with the child—I asked her if it was her child—she said not—I asked where she got it—she said at a A public-house from a man and woman there, who gave her the child to hold, that they went away for half an hour, and did not return—she went out walked about for three quarters of an hour, returned, and asked the land-lord if anybody had inquired for the child—he said no, and she took it home with her to take it to Linfield—in taking her from the station to the Union workhouse, she said if I wanted to know anything, she would give me a great deal of trouble, and I might go and find it out
----— HALE. I am master of Croydon workhouse. On Thursday afternoon, the 31st of March, the officer brought the prisoner there with the child—she gave her name Mary Ann Dunn—it was my duty to enter her name and the child's name in the admission book—I asked her about the child—self said it was her own—I asked her what name I should put down—was it christened?"—she said it was no name—I said I must enter it as Dunn only—she said "No, don't enter it as Dunn, it is not my child"—she seemed very much excited at the time, and afterwards she seemed very penitent, and said she would tell me the truth—that she had been very much excited by being questioned by so many, but the fact was she was in a public-house drinking with some people, they got tipsy together, and that the mother gave her the child to hold while she went out—she did not return in half an hour, and she went out for about half an hour looking for her—she returned again, and the landlord said nobody had been inquiring about the child, and she said, "Unfortunately, not knowing what to do, I took it down to my parents at Linfield, I was taken befor a Magistrate, who said I had better return to London, and I was returning when I was taken into custody.
WILLIAM HENRY COLLINS . I keep the Sugar-loaf public-house, King-street, Drury-lane. I remember the prrisoner, the mother of the child, and two men, coming into my house on the 29th of March—the prosecutrix was carrying the child—they were there two hours and a half—then a soldier came in—they might be a little fresh, all of them, but not intoxicated after they were in some time a fiddler came in and played, but I stopped him—I remember the prosecutrix coming and calling out for her child—the prisoner was gone then and the child—the prisoner never came to make any inquiry afterwards.
Prisoner's Defence. She was quite tipsy. I was not in liquor at first. I was in company of a young man at the time—the mother was showing the child, and put it over the table. I said, "What age is it?" she said three weeks. I looked at the young man with me and said, "Three weeks old, and the mother tipsy;" she said, "Will you take it?" I took it in my arms; she said, "Come with me to the Sugar Loaf public-house." I went there with her; she and I were out together; she had the child in her arms afterwards, and when we came back the husband was gone out; she said to me, "Take hold of the child." I took it, and she never came back to me. I sat there half an hour, and said I should not stop there any longer, and I went home. I did not know what to do with the child; she has told a great many stories, but that is the truth.
ANN POPE . I am the prisoner's mother. She has had the misfortune
have fits ever since she was four years, old, and at times is very queer—since the death of her brother she has been a great deal worse.
NOT GUILTY .

1382. DAVID RENTON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April 1 watch value 3 l.; I watch-key, value 10.; and 1 watch-guard, value 10 s.; the goods of Archibald Mackinnon, from his person.
ARCHIBALD MACKINNON . I am private in the 72nd Hinhlanders. On the 4th of April I was at the George and Dragon public-house in Hollabnd-street—I was drunk, and went up to bed—the prisoner went up stairs with me—I had a watch, key, and chain with me—the key now produced is mine—the watch has not been found—the key was fastened to the silver guard—the prisoner did not sleep with me.
prisoner. Q. Had you the watch on you when you went to bed. A. I cannot say that I had.
RICHARD PHELPS . I am shopman to Mr. Aldous, a pawnbroker, in Greek-street, Soho. This watch-key was pawned, by the prisoner on Tuesday morning, the 5th of April, between eight o'clock and half-past, for 1 s.
prisoner. Q. About how long would it take me to come from Holland-street and return again? A. Hardly two minutes.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of it. I was at the foot of the stairs with the sergeant of his party, there were seven or eight persons with him, that evening, and it might be some of them that did it. I saw no watch in his possession that night; there are a good many keys like that.
ARCHIBALD MACKINNON re-examined. I can swear to the key—it has two stones in it, one red and the other blue, and there is a little hole or scratch on the red stone, by which I know it—the prisoner has two stripes on his arm, which shows that he cannot be a man of bad character.

1384. JOSEPH MOORE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JAMES POTTER . I live in Tyson-street, Bethnal-green—I was in the employ of Samuel Parker Noble, of Charles-street Fitzroy-square. On the 7th of April last year the prisoner came and said, Will you send a date, of stout to Mr. Tulk, of Duke-street, Westminster?—I said, "Yes"—before he left he said, "Will you lend me 10 s., it will be returned when the stout is sent to Mr. Tulk's"—I spoke to Mr. Noble, and he lent him the 10 s.—he is not here—I had never seen the prisoner before—he said he had come out without any money, and he had to buy-something for the young ladies—I supposed he meant Mr. Tulk's daughters—he did not say so.
WILLIAM PRICE . I received the stout at Mr. Tulk's from Mr. Noble's man—it was brought in a cart.
WILLIAM WYBROO . I am a cheese monger, and live in Bridge-street, Westminster. On the 3rd of November last the prisoner came to me—I knew him—he said he came from Miss Page's for a piece of bacon—I have served the Miss Page's—he said, "The cook says you know the part that will suit them, and if you have not any one with in to send it immediately, I will take it with me"—in consequence of that statement I let him have the bacon, which I should not have done without—the Miss Pages are siters to the Rev. Mr. Page, and live with him—last Saturday three weeks the prisoner came to my shop again and said, "You serve the Rev. Mr. Page, don't you?"—I said, "Yes, I do, you mean the Rev. Mr. Page, living in the cloisters?"—he said, "Yes, I am living with him, he has sent me to order half a ham, three or four pounds of good Cheshire cheese, and 1 s. worth of eggs, he is going to have company to-morrow, and wishes them to be particularly good—I want to see them"—I cut him off some cheese, showed him half a ham, and asked how long he had lived with Mr. Page—he said between eight and nine months—I did not let him have those things—I should not have let him had the bacon if he had not represented that he came from the Miss Pages—I charged for it when I sent in the bill, and they would not pay it.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear I came to your shop on the 3rd of November; you told the Magistrate it was a man like me? A. I hare not the slightest doubt you are the man.
THE REV. CYRIL WILLIAM PAGE . I have some sisters, who are called the Miss Pages—the prisoner was in my service at the beginning of last year for three weeks—he was not in my service on the 3rd of November last, or at any time in November—he had no authority from me or my sisters to get bacon for us.
Prisoner's Defence. I am very sorry for what has occurred, but Mr. Wybroo swears falsely. I was in Warwickshire at the time.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18420404-1385]
1385. LOUISA BIRCH was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM ALDRIGE . I lodged in Easton-street, Clerkenwell, on the first-floor—my bed-room was underneath a room occupied by the prisoner—she lived there about nine or ten weeks while I was there—my work kept me at home all day—there was a child in her room, but I did not know it was hers at that time—I did not see it till the parish officers came—I knew it was in the room, by its constantly crying from morning till night—the prisoner generally went out between six and seven o'clock in the morning, but on Monday mornings between four and five, and returned about seven or eight in the evening—she would then go up stairs and dress herself—some evenings she would be at home, and sometimes she would be out till one or two in the morning—during the time she was away no one was in the room with the child—I have sometimes gone up and tried the door, but always found it locked, and no person there—after this had gone on some time, the child, instead of crying loud as it used to do, so that you could hear it from the top to the bottom of the house, was constantly moaning—I believe the prisoner used generally to be at home on Sundays, as far as I know—the child used to cry, if possible, more when she was at home than when she was out—I never heard her speak to the
child in any consoling way—the only expression I ever heard her use was as I was coming down stairs once, when she cried out, "Oh you little brute, "or" You noisy little brute"—the child was about two years old—a young man used to come and see the prisoner—I have seen him come on a Saturday night, and sometimes he stopped all Sunday—I have heard him go out with the prisoner between four or five on Monday morning—several gentlemen besides the young man used to come and see her—they came when she used to come home of an evening—I do not know whether they came home with her—I have known them go out together afterwards—she generally appeared to be very lively, and was constantly singing up and down stairs as late as one or two o'clock in the morning—she was not instarving state, far from it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Why do you say far from it? A. In appearance—I do not know what she had to live on, or to eat—I am a wine and spirit finer—I took my meals with a person named Hoare, who kept the first-floor, but I slept in the first-floor back-room alone—the child used to cry from the time the prisoner went out till she returned—I cannot say that it was constantly crying, but for five or six hours, and that continued for nine or ten weeks—I never spoke to the prisoner about it—I complained to the people of the house, how scandalous it was for the child to be so neglected, and at last went to the overseers of the parish—I did not like to interfere in it before, being a man, and I was not aware that the child was neglected so much as it was—she appeared flighty in her manner when she was singing up and down stairs—I have not heard that she was insane for three months about the time of the child's birth—I heard the man who passed as her husband state, that she was very bad at the time of her confinement.
SARAH THRIFT . I am married, and life on the second-floor at No. 14, Easton-street, Clerkenwell, in the next room to the prisoner—she lodged there with the child for about nine or ten weeks—I have heard the child cry repeatedly—the prisoner used to go out between six and seven o'clock in the morning, and return about the same time in the evening, and sometimes went out again after that—I believe no one was left in the room with the child while she was out—I have tried about three times to look into the room when she has been going down stairs, to see whether there was any one there, but she always shut the door—she did not take the key with her when she only went down stairs—I did not open the door to look in—when she went away for the day she locked the door, and I had no means of looking to see what was the matter with the child—I never remember any person being left with the child when she went out—I never heard her speak kind to it, whenever she did speak she called it a little devil—that was when she has been going into the yard—it was a female child—I remember the parish-officers coming to the place—the child was in the room—after the first gentleman had been the prisoner came to my door, and asked if I knew any thing of a little girl that would take care of a child—I told her I did not—I afterwards went with the child to the workhouse.
ELIZABETH FORD . I lived in the first-floor front-room of this house—I knew the prisoner living there nine or ten weeks—I never saw the child till it was taken away by the parish-officers—I have several times token up letters and messages on purpose to go in, but I never did—she was not at home, and I found the door locked—I have tried the door several times—I heard the child cry very much indeed, several times—I do
not know whether the prisoner was out at those times, but I never could get in—I never saw any one left to take care of the child—the prisoner used to come home about eight or nine o'clock in the evening and went out after that—I have let her in several times at twelve or one o'clock, and the last time was half-past two—I have twice seen her with a pound of bread and a bundle of wood at night—I never saw any thing else.
Cross-examined. Q. She was always sober, was she not? A. Yes—she paid 1 s. 9 d. a week rent.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What sort of spirits used she to he in? A. Very good indeed—we often used to wonder how she could sing as she did, and her baby be crying so.
MARY ANN METCALF . I lodged in this house—I was unwell at the time, and confined to my bed—the child was crying from morning till night.
WILLIAM TAYLOR . I am surgeon of the parish of Clerkenwell. I was called in by, desire of the parish-officer to see the child—it appeared to be about eighteen months old—it was very much emaciated when first admitted into the house—the spine was curved, the radius of both arms bent—the mesenteric glands were very enlarged—it appeared to me those symtoms were in a great measure caused by continued neglect and want of proper sustenance and care—it being left alone from ten to twelve hours without the attendance of any person would be very likely to produce those appearances—there is a disease of the mesenteric glands to which children are subject, which would cause similar appearances—in such a case you may fill a child to repletion, and yet it would die of starvation, but from the rapid improvement of this child when supplied with proper nonrishment, I think it was not the case here—in organic disease of the mesenteric glands that improvement would not take place—I did not see the child in the room.
Cross-examined. Q. When a child is brought up by hand, the mother being insane and unable to attend to it, does it not sometimes get curvature of the spine? A. Sometimes—I should recommend as a remedy that the child should sometimes lie flat on its back on something hard—there appeared to be no disease of the lungs—I could not detect any.
SARAH GREEN . I live in Exmouth-street. The house in which the prisoner lived is my husband's—two or three days before the parish-officers took the child away, she came to pay her rent, which was 1 s. 9 d. a week—I asked her on that occasion how it was that the child cried so much, and whether she left any one with it—she said she left a girl with it—I asked if the child was ill—she said it was, that she had taken it to a doctor, and it had eaten half a-pound of meat the night before—I said, "Half a pound of meat?"—she said the doctor had ordered nourishment—I said I thought it was wrong to give it so much—I never saw the child.
Cross-examined. Q. You thought what she said to you very strange? A. Yes.
CHARLES ALLBERRY . I am assistant clerk to the Guardians of Clerkenwell parish. On Friday, the 4th of March, in consequence of complaints having been made to Mr. Stevens, the overseer, I went to the prisoner's room—she was at home—when I knocked at the room door, she kept her knee against the door, and refused me admittance—I said I said I was a parish officer, that there were great complaints made of the way in which she treated her child, which had not been seen by anybody, and I was determined
to see it—she said, "You shall not, I have a right to manage my child as I like"—I said, "I agree with that, if you are doing justice to the child, and if so, you can have no objection to my seeing it"—she kept her knee against the door, and said I should not see it—I said my time was precious, I must see it, and if its appearance was satisfactory I should report accordingly—I then forced my way in, and found a respectably dressed man sitting down to tea and buttered toast, and every thing seemed as comfortable as any one could wish—I said, "Where is the child?"—she went and sat down on one side of the fire-place—I said to the man, "I presume you are her husband"—he said, "No, I am not, I was merely passing by and looked in"—I said, "Where is the child?"—after some hesitation she produced it from a corner of the room—it was lying on the floor, not in a bed—I lifted up the clothes, and the sight was beyond any thing I had ever seen—the legs were not thicker than my two fingers, and it was in the most awfully emaciated state—I said, "This is too bad, what is the reason of the child's being in this state?"—she said it was disease—I said, "Have you any one attending it?"—she said, "No, I have not"—I said, "Why not?"—she said, "Because it is useless"—I said, "If you are doing justice by it, you can have no objection to the parish doctor seeing it"—she hesitated, and said she had a right to manage her child as she pleased—I said, "The doctor shall see it, I will go and write an order immediately"—I then left—in the course of the evening Mr. Stevens went and removed it to the workhouse, and the surgeon saw it.
COURT. Q. Although the child was in this emaciated state, did it appear to be clean, tidy, and wholesome? A. It was not clean, nor yet out of the way dirty—it was dressed, and lying on the bare floor—there was a bundle in the corner—it appeared like a bed with a kind of coverlid thrown over.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there any bedstead? A. No—the prisoner was very respectably dressed, in a bombazeen dress—I heard before the Magistrate that she comes from Clifton.
WILLIAM MALLETT . I am chief clerk at the police-court, Clerkenwell. I attended at the examination of the prisoner on this charge—she made a statement, which I took down accurately in writing—it was not signed by her—this is it—( reads)—"The prisoner says the child was christened Selina Emily Highton, and I never called it' brute or devil' in my life; it was christened at Clifton Church."
I am a nurse at the workhouse. I received this letter from the prisoner in the workhouse, after she was taken into custody, for the purpose of forwarding it—( read)—directed "Mr. J. Highton, Mr. Spikes, grocer, Lower Edmonton, near London; with speed. My dear chicken, —I hardly know what I am writing about, I am nearly writing frantic; the people in the house where I am living, have been to the parish officers and complained that the child was locked up all day; of course I am going before the Magistrates this morning; four of them came up into my room, and all the people in the house; it almost terrified me to death; I do not know what will be the; I was obliged to say I was married, or they would confine me three months for having a chance child; do not, pray, should they send to you, contradict it, or I shall weak my heart. Oh, my dear chicken, what shall I, shall I do; I knew this would be the consequence; I wish I had staid at home and minded
her, if it had been on bread and water; they will take her into the work-house, and make you pay 1 s. 6 d. a week with her; they won't take my word; do, my dear chicken, oh do come up, for I shall not be liberated until; I can assure you I am almost mad; they have left me in charge of an officer; you must come to the New Police-office, Police-court, Bagnigge-road, Clerkenwell; do, for God's sake, come; I have been insulted grossly insulted, by the officer; I feel it very deeply, and wounded to the very heart. I am yours. What shall I do if they think your name is Davis?"
MR. PAYNE called the following witnesses.
SARAH LEWIS . I am the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Lewis, who are now at Manchester. The prisoner came to London from Bristol, the latter end of November, or the beginning of December—I was in the habit of seeing her from time to time—my son and his wife gave her a residence under their own roof, until she saw the father of the child—(she came to London with the expectation of seeing the father)—she resided with them till business was so bad they were obliged to leave London, and then the went to this room—during the time she was with my son, she behaved to the child with the greatest kindness, as far as I saw, and she was particularly partial to any one that would do any little thing for it—I never saw her at Easton-street, nor since she left my sons, until yesterday.
MR. BODKIN. Q. How old was the child then? A. I think about eighteen months—it was not in good health—it was a delicate baby—she behaved as a natural mother to it—she nursed it, gave it proper food, and did not leave it—she had no occasion to do so at that time—she seemed to understand how to treat it.
MR. PAYNE. Q. She had no employment at that time? A. No—if she was obliged to go out, there were persons in the house to take care of the child.
SARAH LEWIS . I am the daughter of last witness. I saw the prisoner when she was living at my brother's, and slept with her and the child for upwards of a week—she was very kind to it—she used not to nurse it much because the doctor ordered it to lie on the floor on its back—I saw her when she was in Easton-street—I slept there from the Friday till the Monday—the child was ill—she said it was like that from three months old, that she put it out to nurse, and the people tied its arras down, and made that deformity in the arms—when I saw her in Easton-street she was very kind to it, the same as usual—she bad only been in the room two nights when I went to sleep with her.
Q. Can you account for her going out in the morning and leaving it all day? A. She was obliged to, to get work, and she had but 5 s. a week—I pledged some things for her—from what I saw, she treated the child well—it was ill, and used to pine and cry all the time.
ELIZA TAYLOR . I am a shirt-maker, and live in New-court, Old Bailey. I employed the prisoner about the 10th of January, and she continued to work for me about eight or ten weeks—she came at eight o'clock in the morning, and staid till seven at night, never later, but she has frequently taken home four or five hours' work at a time, and done it at home with her own hands, I am certain, and nicely done—I never saw the child till she was taken up—she could not earn more than 6 s. or 7 s. a week, working as hard as she could, needle-work is very badly paid for—I know that she
had nothing to eat herself, but dry bread, while at my house—she always conducted herself with propriety while with me.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Of course you were not aware that she had men come to see her of an evening? A. I was not—I knew she had a child at borne—I concluded that she left some one to take care of it—I believe she told me so—I have no doubt she did—she said it was attended to by a person who she left at home to take care of it while she came to work.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined One Year.
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18420404-1387]
1387. THOMAS NICHOLLS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of March, 1 10 l. Bank-note, and 25 l. Bank-notes, the property of James Mays, from his person.
JAMES MAYS . On the 2nd of March I received from Mr. Liddle, of Woodford, two 5 l. notes, and a 10 l. note—I went to the White Hart public-house, kept by Mr. Stokes, about eleven o'clock in the forenoon, I there got drunk—my money was in my left-hand breeches' pocket, in a canvass bag—I fell asleep between one and two—I awoke up about half-past three, and my money was then gone—the bag was still in my pocket—the prisoner was in the room, but not with me.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you been drinking? A. I had had two glasses of rum-and-water—no more, to my knowledge—I will not swear it—two glasses of rum-and-water will make me drunk—I paid two sixpences for them—I was in no other public-house before that—I did not toss my Bank-notes about on the table—I had no silver in my bag—I had a sovereign—the prisoner was the only person in the worn when I went to sleep—I put my money into this little bag—I had never known the prisoner before—I will swear that he was quite a stranger to me—I never spoke to him to my knowledge—I never was in this public-house above five times during the seven years I have been at Woodford—I did not get drunk—I was wet through, and sitting over the fire made me sleepy—I had a sixpenny-worth to drink after I awoke—I was nearly as sober as I am now—I did not swear just now that I was drunk—I might have been a little fresh, but I was not drunk—I did not give the prisoner my money to take care of—I am a bailiff to a gentleman—I have no recollection of ever drinking with the prisoner in my life—I have only been drinking a glass of ale to day with my dinner.
GEORGE LIDDLE . I am Post-master of Woodford. About four o'clock in the afternoon of the 2nd of March, I gave the prosecutor a 10 l. and two 5 l. Bank of England notes—I have not got the numbers of them—one of the endorsements was the 5th of February, and one the 26th of February
—I did not give the Magistrate the number of the 10 l. note—I never had the note in my hand after I gave it to Mays.
THOMAS CLARK . I was at the Castle at Woodford, about three o'clock on the 2nd of March—the prisoner came in and had some beer—I saw something lying on the table like a packet of papers—I took it up, thinking it was a bit of waste-paper—one piece of it fell down—I looked at it, and it was a 5 l. note—soon after I picked up another piece and found that to be a 5 l. note—I handed them round the room to see if any one owned them, but at that time no one did—the prisoner was in the room at the time—I should fancy he could hear me ask for the owner—there were only four persons in the room—he pulled a key from his pocket, and I observed what appeared to be another note, squeezed up in the same manner as the other two were—he said, "Has any one found anything?"—I said, "I think I have"—he said, "I have lost some money"—I said, "What have you lost?"—he said, "A 10 l. note, and a 5 l, note"—I said, "Then, they can't be yours"—he said, "Perhaps there may be two five's, I am not positive"—I said, "Do you claim this as your property?"—he said, "I do"—and I immediately handed them to him—he was drunk.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know the prosecutor? A. Merely by sight—I have seen him once or twice before this at the Castle—whenever I did see him there he was drunk.
RICHARD BYRNE . I keep the Castle, at Woodford. The prisoner came to my house about four o'clock on Wednesday, the 2nd of March—he asked for some beer and tobacco—I brought it in—he asked if I wanted the money for it—he said he could not pay me, would I let him have it upon that, and gave me this 5 l. note as security for the beer—he said a relation had died and left him the money, and this was the first instalment. cross-examined. Q. Had you known him before? A. For sometime—I believe he is a tailor—I had only been in the house about four months—the beer came to 1 s. 8 d.—he was in the habit of frequenting my house—he lives in the village—my house is about half a mile from the White Hart—I could have found him out at any time—I should say he was a little tipsy—I did not see him long.
JOSEPH CRICKS ( police-constable K 331.) I received information from Mays, and went to the public-house where the prisoner was—I asked him where the notes were which he had taken from Mays at the White Hart public-house—he said they were in his pocket, and pulled out the notes—I asked him to give them to me—he said he would not—I said I must have them—I took hold of his hand, and took from his hand two notes, one was a 10 l. note—I asked him where the other one was—he said it was all right, Mays had given him them to take care of—I cautioned him not to say more to me, as it would be made use of before the Magistrate—in taking him to the station, he said Mays had lent him the money, and would lend him more if he wanted it.
Cross-examined. Q. Were not both the prisoner and prosecutor tipsy?. A. They were—I have known the prisoner ever since I have been at Woodford—I never heard any thing against his character.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420404-1388]
1388. JOHN ALLEN, MAJOR HENNESEY, JOHN PARKER , and WILLIAM WINDLEY were indicted for stealing, on the 24th off March, 1 pair of trowsers, value 2 s. 6 d.; I pair of stockings, value 1 s.; I shirt, value 1 s.; the goods of the guardians of the poor of the West Ham Union: and 3 sheets, value 1 s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of William Arkill.
JOHN HIGHAM . I am master of the West Ham Union Workhouse—the four prisoners were discharged from there on Thursday morning, the 24th of March—in consequence of circumstances, I went into the laundry on Friday morning, the 25th of March, between ten and eleven o'clock, and missed from there three shirts, a pair of stockings, and a handkerchief—I traced some footmarks over the back fence into the Infirmary-yard, and from there into the laundry—none of the articles were found—I gave information to the police—Allen was brought in in the afternoon—I ordered him to be locked up till we traced the footmarks—he made a statement about others, but nothing about himself.
MICHAEL CASEY . I am a policeman. I took Allen—in taking him before the master of the Union, he said that he received the goods out of the laundry from some one—that there were three sheets, a handkerchief, and a pair of stockings—he said he stood outside the laundry window—he did not say how far he was from the laundry—he said he accompanied other to London to dispose of them—they have not been found.
GEORGE SMITH . I am a police-sergeant. I went to Windley's house, and found him concealed under the stairs—I took him to the station—I took off his, Allen's, and Parker's shoes—the next morning I went to the Union, and saw a great many marks which correspond with Allen's and Parker's marks—I could not see any of Windley's—Hennesey was not in custody then—I put the shoes into the holes, and they exactly fitted.
GEORGE CARTER . I am a policeman. About half-past nine o'clock on Friday morning, I was on duty at Low Leyton, and saw the four prisoners coming in the direction from the Union—Parker had a bundle—Parker, Windley, and Hennesey took the road leading to Temple Mills—Allen went towards Stratford—after going some short distance, Hennesey called to Allen, and told him to wait at Stratford, and they would meet him there—I afterwards had suspicion, and followed, but did not overtake them.
HENRY MILES . I am a policeman. I apprehended Hennesey on Saturday morning, the 26th of March—I told him be was wanted for the robbery at the Union about the sheets—he asked me who we had locked up—I mentioned Allen's, Parker's, and Windley's names—he said, "It will be a gooser with some of us"—I then took him to the station.
ROBERT REYNOLDS . Between eight and nine o'clock on the 24th of March, Windley and another came to my house, and called for a pint of porter, they stopped a few minutes, and went out into the street.
Allen's Defence. All Casey says against me is false; I was not near the place.
Parker's Defence. My shoes were taken off my feet; they could not correspond, for I was not on the premises from the time I left.
NOT GUILTY .
KENT CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420404-1389]
1389. PETER LOCKETT , ANN MERRY , and CATHERINE JONES were indicted for a robbery, on the 30th of March, on John Knott putting him in corporal fear and danger of his life, and stealing violently from his person, and against his will, 1 tobacco-box, value 3 d,; I sixpence, 2 pence, 2 halfpence, and 2 farthings; the property of the said John Knott, and immediately after the robbery aforesaid, beating, striking, and using other personal violence to him.
JOHN KNOTT . I am a painter, and live in the Broadway, Deptford. On the 30th of March, about a quarter after two o'clock in the morning, I was in New Cross-road, in the parish of St. Paul's, Deptford—I met the three prisoners—they all three passed me, and then Merry returned, and said, "My dear, where are you going?"—I said, "I am going home"—she said, "You had better go along with me"—I said, "Be off about your busmen, I have nothing to say to you"—the prisoner Jones came behind me, and raised the tail of my coat pocket—I said,"There is nothing at all there, be off about your business"—they followed me down the road, about sixty or seventy yards—when they found I would have nothing to do with them, Merry put her hand into my left trowsers' pocket—Jones put her hand into my right-hand pocket—I had a tobacco-box in my left-hand pocket, and in my right 6 d., and at least three penny-pieces, two or three halfpence, and two farthings—I hollooed out "Police," and then the prisoner Lockett came up, and struck me over the ear, and knocked me down—it was a very severe blow, and he made an attempt at my watch—I happened to have my chain round my brace, and with pulling it he could not get my watch out of my pocket—some people were coming along, one of whom, Mr. Bond, I knew—I said, "Mr. Bond, is that you?"—he said, "What is the matter?" I said, "These people have been robbing me"—he said, "Which is it?"—I said, "All of them have tried, and two of them have robbed me"—Lockett made a kick at Bond, and I believe Bond struck him—a policeman came up, and after that I missed my tobacco-box, 6 d., the three penny-pieces and the two farthings from my two pockets—I gave them all three into the custody of the policeman—my tobacco-box was produced at the station.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. This was on the night of Green-which fair, was it not? A. Yes, I had been at the Five Bells public-house, Kent-road, where there was a dance—I was going down to Deptford when I met them—I had been drinking—Bond had not been with me—I had not seen him for above twelve months—I did not see him strike one of the women—I was near him—I will swear he did not strike one of them—we had had two pints of porter, and three glasses of gin-and-water between three of us—that was between half-past seven and two o'clock—the prisoners were altogether when they passed me, going in a contrary way, coming up from Greenwich—they passed me, and then turned back—Lockett did not interfere till I refused to go with the young women—they followed me sixty or seventy yards—he must have been close behind—as soon as hollooed out "Police," he knocked me down—after the woman took my money and box out, I called out "Police," and Lockett came up, struck me, and knocked me down—he struck me at the side of my ear—I could see him, for he came right on the side of me to do so, he came from behind
Bond is a plasterer—I do not know whether he is a fighting man—he is a strong, stout man.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. This part of the road is not very well lighted, is it? A. Yes, there are gas-lights in the road—they are some distance from each other—I am certain it was Merry that spoke to me—when they first passed me they did not speak at all—I walked several yards and she came up by herself at that time—I told her be off, and then Jones came up—I said nothing to Jones till I said there was nothing there, meaning in my pocket, when she put her hand in—as soon as one's hand was out of my pocket the other put her's in—I had two half-crowns in my waistcoat pocket, that I did not lose—when I first met them there was a tallish man about twenty yards a-head of them—there were not a number of people coming from Greenwich—I did not meet a soul till Bond came up—the prisoners appeared all together—I paid particular attention to them—they were closer together than they are now—I cannot say they were talking together—I saw them before they came up to me—they were walking together, and came back after me.
CHARLES HEATH BOND . I am a plasterer, and live at Peckham. On the 30th of March I was in the New Cross-road, coming from Blackheath—I heard Mr. Knott call out "Police"—I went up to him directly, and saw the two female prisoners with him—the prisoner Lockett was there, and he struck Knott—that was after the cry of police—I went op to Mr. Knott, and Lockett pulled off his coat and said, "What do you want, I will fight you"—I said, "I don't want any of your fighting"—he directly up with his foot and kicked me in the * * *—I immediately collared him—he directly took a box out of his pocket, and chucked over the hedge —I said, "There goes something he has taken out of his pocket"—the policeman immediately came up—I told the policeman he had chucked something over the hedge—he gave me his lantern, and I went through the hedge, and a young lad with me, who picked up the tobacco-box in my presence, in Mr. Emmett's field—they were taken to the station—before the box was found I struck Jones, as she pulled her bonnet off and came and struck at me as if to scratch me—my nose has been sore ever since—she cut me inside the nose with her fist—she was taking the part of Lockett who had kicked me—when I struck him, she struck me.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What are you? A. A plasterer, a task master—I take contracts from builders—I told the Magistrate about Lockett kicking me—what I said was read over to me—I put my mark to it—this is it. ( The witness's deposition being read, stated, "I went up, saw two young women, some words are, I struck Jones, Lockett then came up, struck me, & c.")—Lockett kicked me before the girl touched me—I did not tell the Magistrate about her making my nose sore—I did not think of it—I told him she struck me, but I did not say it was on my nose—I had only had three pints of porter all day—I had been over to a gentleman on the heath, who was very ill—I was coiming from Blackheath—I had not been near the fair—I am not a fighter—I have been in scuffles at public houses, but never in a regular fight—I struck Lockett after he kicked me—I never struck him on the collar-bone—I believe it was somewhere in the face—he ran behind a cab, and fell down on the path of his own accord, and hurt his collar-bone—he did not fall from my blow, but stood afterwards and struck at me—I told him I did not wish to hurt him, but to give him in charge—I did not strike him 11 he kicked me.
Jones. Q. Who was it called the police? A. Mr. Knott. Jones. No, it was me—I received a blow and a kick and called the police—you kicked me yourself, and told me next day if I said nothing about that, you would say nothing against me. Witness. It is not true—I never saw you till I came up.
MR. PAYNE. Q. You say when you came up Lockett struck Knott, what was the effect of that blow? A. He struck him on the ear-he might have struck him before for what I know—there was one or two person in the road.
WILLIAM KING ( police-constable R 205.) On the 30th of March I was on the New Cross-road, and noticed a disturbance—I went up—Knott gave Lockett in custody—I received a tobacco-box from a boy, who went over the hedge with Bond, to whom I lent my lantern—I took Lockett to the station, searched him, found a sovereign, a half-crown, and one shil—in his stocking, and 2 s. 10 1/4 d. in his pocket, and two handkerchiefs—one farthing was very black on one side, and worn more bright on the other, it had not been described to me before I found it on him, but before it was shown to Knott he described it to me—I did not find a sixpence on him—the women followed him to the station and were then taken into custody—Knott did not say that they had anything to do with it till he got to the station—he then told me how it occurred—he said Lockett had attempted to take his watch, and he had lost his tobacco-box.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. The women followed after you quietly enough, they never offered to run away? A. No.
COURT. Q. What did you notice before you got up to them? A. I heard a noise, and something like a cry of "Police," and a disturbance—I ran down the road, and Knott said, "I give this young man in charge, he has knocked me down, and they have robbed me"—the women were by at the time, all in the affray—the prosecutor owned the tobacco-box—it was given him by the boy—he came through the hedge with it.
JOHN KNOTT ( re-examined.) I will swear to this farthing being the one I lost—I had had it in my pocket on Monday and Tuesday—I brought it from home—I took it for some beer at the Druid's Head, and had it in my pocket ever since Monday—this was Wednesday morning.
(Lockett received a good character.)
LOCKETT- GUILTY . Aged 18. MERRY- GUILTY . Aged 18.
Confined One Year.
JONES— GUILTY . Aged 29.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18420404-1390]
1390. WILLIAM GODFREY was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 59 1/2 lbs. weight of lead, value 4 s. 6 d., the goods of Anthony Strother, then being fixed to a building.—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.
JAMES SALTER . I am gardener to Mr. Gillett, of Shooter's-hill. On the 25th of March I noticed some lead was taken off the roof of the water-closet of a house belonging to Mr. Strother, at Shooter's-hill—I examined the roof—the lead had been cut by some sharp instrument—I have compared some lead with what remained on the building, and am satisfied it is part of what had been on the premises—it fitted in every direction with what remained on the water-closet.
JOSEPH CAPON ( police-constable R 112.) On the 25th of March, about half-past ten o'clock in the morning, I was on duty in Dover-road, near Shooters's-hill,
about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's premises, and saw the prisoner coming towards London with a bundle—he sat down on the side of the bank—I went up to him, and asked what he had in his possession—he said be believed it was nothing but his own property—he said he came from Gravesend—I asked him what it was—he said, "A lump of lead"—I asked where he got it—he said he bought it of a man for 2 s. about two miles on the road—I took him to the station, and found a robbery had been committed at Shooter's-hill—I compared the lead with the water-closet, and it fitted in every respect—I am quite satisfied the lead came from the building—it made up the entire quantity taken.
CHARLES CHAMBERLAIN . I am in the employ of Cockrell and Son, auctioneers, and live in Redcross-street, London. On the 25th of March I saw the prisoner in the Bull public-house, at Shooter's-hill, between six and seven o'clock in the morning—I had seen him the night before on the lawn in front of the premises the lead was stolen from—there had been a sale there, and he assisted in moving articles from the sale.
Prisoner. I was never on the premises. Witness. I am quite certain of him—he had a jacket swung round him, and he was apprehended with the lead rolled up in that jacket—( produced)—this is it. JOSEPH CAPON re-examined. He had the lead swung in the jacket.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not near the premises; I bought it as I came along the road that morning.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Three Months.

1393. DANIEL SHEEN was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 16 1/2 lbs, weight of nails, value 13 s., the goods of Joseph Soames.
STEPHEN WHARTON . I am a mariner of the Adelaide transport, belonging to Joseph Soames—the prisoner was at work on that ship. On the 30th of March he came into the cabin, where I was making out accounts—I saw him go out with a large bundle of canvass—it appeared to me as though it contained something—I watched him, and saw him go into the cabin—he afterwards went on the other side of the deck—I went into
the bow cabin, and found concealed in a corner, a bundle of canvass containing a quantity of composition nails, such as we were using to copper the ship with—it exactly corresponded with the bundle the prisoner had carried out—there was a great quantity of nails in the place from whence this bundle was taken—there was such an immense quantity that I could not tell whether any had been gone—I afterwards went on shore and acquainted Mr. Soames, the owner—he gave me some directions, in consequence of which, after the prisoner had gone on shore we went to tee if any nails were gone, and they were—in the meantime Mr. Soames had met him and stopped him—I got up to him—he then went and brought a policeman, but previously to the policeman coming, the prisoner threw a bundle of nails out of his pocket—I believe them to be the same nails at Mr. Soames had, belonging to the ship.
Prisoner. I was at work on board the ship—the spun-yarn lay in the cabin where the nails were—I fetched a bit of spun-yarn out—the nails were given me by the cook to use. Witness. He stated at Greenwich, that they were given him by the mate, and he is here—if they had been given to him by any body, it was his duty to use them on board the ship, not to throw them away—he threw the nails away about fifty yards from the vessel—these nails produced by the policeman also correspond with the ship's nails.
WILLIAM MOFF ( police-constable M 273.) I took the prisoner into custody, and produce the nails—I found this 7lbs. on his person after I took him to the station—they are the same sort of nails.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not take the nails out of the cabin; I had them for the use of the ship.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.
