1397. WILLIAM BATES and HENRY DOVE were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of Alfred Charles, from his person.
ALFRED CHARLES . I am a draper, living in. Britannia-terrace. On the 19th of March I was in Greenwich-park, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon—an officer came, and asked me whether I had lost any thing—I said, "No," and removed to another part of the park—he asked me' again, and I missed my handkerchief—we then followed the prisoners, and found this handkerchief in Bates's pocket—it is" mine—I had had it safe in my pocket about a quarter of an hour before—Bates did not make any remark till we got to the police-office—he then said I had thrown it to a lady, and he picked it up—I did not throw it to a lady.
JOHN PASSMORE MUMFORD ( police-constable A 35.) I was on duty, and saw the prisoners standing behind the prosecutor—I had seen them speak to each other previously—I saw Bates lay hold of the prosecutor's pocket with his left hand, and take hold of his handkerchief with his right hand—the prosecutor moved away at the moment, and they left him—I afterwards saw them standing behind the prosecutor again—they had followed him to another ring where they were playing at "kiss in the ring"—I saw Bates run away—Thompson spoke to the prosecutor—I then took Dove into custody, and Thompson took Bates—I had seen them in company together for half-an-hour—I did not see them touch any one else's pocket. Dote. We were not behind the gentleman at all; we were ten yards from him; the gentleman chucked the handkerchief to a lady in a black drew; she would not pick it up; they were playing at "kiss in the ring." Witness. In playing at "kiss in the ring," one person throws a handkerchief, and the other picks it up, but I am sure that was not the case here—the prosecutor did not throw the handkerchief to a lady.
JOSEPH THOMPSON ( police-constable F 39.) I saw the two prisoners behind the prosecutor—I saw Dove go before him, and then step back, and Bates went behind him—I saw Bates put his hand under the prosecutor's coat, and then go away—Dove then went behind him—I went to the prosecutor, and asked if he. had lost any thing—he said he had lost his handkerchief—I followed, and saw Dove putting something into his jacket pocket,
and between his waistcoat pocket and the lining I found this handkerchief, which the prosecutor identified.
Bates's Defence. I did not take the handkerchief; the prosecutor chucked it to a lady, and no one would pick it up.
BATES*— GUILTY . Aged 17. DOVE*— GUILTY . Aged 17.
Confined Six Months.

1398. JAMES HUNT and JAMES GRABBIT were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of James Saussey, from his person.
JAMES SAUSSEY . I am a cabinet-maker, and live in Beersland-street, Kingsland-road. I was at Greenwich fair on Monday the 28th of March, and lost my handkerchief there—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. It is a very poor one, is it not? A. Yes—I know the handkerchief by its being full of holes.
CHARLES JACKSON ( police-constable A 126.) I was on duty at Green-which fair—I saw Hunt take the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket, and give it to Grabbit—I immediately took them both into custody—I had been watching them previously—they were both together.
Cross-examined. Q. You are quite sure you saw it? A. Quite sure—I was quite close to them—I was in plain clothes.
Hunt. Q. Did you hold up the handkerchief when you took me, and ask if the bystanders had lost one? A. Yes—I did so to let the prosecutor know he might come to the station.
JOHN PASSMORE MUMFORD . I am a policeman. I was at Greenwich fair—I saw Hunt lay hold of the prosecutor's pocket, take the handkerchief from it, and hand it to Grabbit—I took Hunt, and took him to the station.
(Grabbit received a good character.)
HUNT — GUILTY. Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months. GRABBIT— GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined One Month.

1399. JOHN WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of March, 6 pieces of wood, value 4 d., the goods of Andrew Hughesdon.
ANDREW HUGHESDON . I am a timber and firewood-dealer. GEORGE SOLE ( police-constable R 174.) On the 3rd of March, about half-past seven o'clock, I met the prisoner in company with another, with some wood under their arms—I turned, and followed him into Barns-alley—when they found I was getting close, they began to run, and threw this wood down—I called to a man to mind it for me.
Cross-examined. Q. Who was carrying the wood? A. Each was carrying some under his arm—I have not got the other person—there were six pieces altogether—Edds was with me—I met him accidentally—he is a Custom-house officer.
WILLIAM EDDS examined by MR. PAYNE. I saw the prisoner and another
lad carrying; some wood—I heard the wood fall—I saw them run, and throw it down—I do not know who it was. GUILTY . Aged 17. Confined One Month.

1400. GEORGE THOMAS was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 3 d., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
JOHN BOVIS ( police-constable R 139.) On Tuesday, the 29th of March, I was in Greenwich fair, in plain clothes—I saw the prisoner there—he was pointed out to me by a respectable tradesman, of Deptford—I watched him, and saw him put his hand into a man's pocket, and take out this handkerchief—I took it out of his hand and laid bold of his collar—he then escaped—I pursued him down the fair—I took him again about seven o'clock the next evening—I do not know the person from whom the handkerchief was taken—I gave him a push, he turned round and looked back, but I had no opportunity of speaking to him.
Prisoner. He said at the station that he knocked my hat off and picked the handkerchief off the ground; I could not find my hat. Witness. I did not say so—he did lose his hat.
JAMES ARCHER . I am a baker at Deptford—I was at Greenwich fair on Tuesday night, and saw the prisoner—he looked very uneasy—I was looking at Richardson's show—I turned round and looked at him—he was very busy trying several gentlemen's pockets—he attempted several gentlemen first who were together, and then two or three single gentlemen—I went down the fair, and in about ten minutes-met the policeman, and gave him information—we came back together into the fair, and in about half an hour fell in with the prisoner—I pointed him out to the policeman—there was a gentleman standing looking at a show—as soon as the gentleman stopped, the prisoner went behind him and began to feel his pockets—I walked to the side of the gentleman—the policeman was behind us—presently some one gave the gentleman a push—I turned round and saw the policeman close to the prisoner—he was too quick for the policeman and got away from him—we pursued him down the fair—he was not taken that evening—the next evening the policeman called for me to go up and recognize him.
Prisoner. He said at the office that he saw me from a quarter past seven o'clock till a quarter past ten, picking gentlemen's pockets; why did he not take roe then? I can swear I was not there. Witness. I said, "Making the attempt"—if I had seen him pull the handkerchiefs out I should have charged him.
Prisoner's Defence. All day on Tuesday I was at the Three Compasses public-house in East-lane, Walworth, and then went home to bed; I was at home all day on Wednesday, as it was wet; I went down with a young woman to the fair, the policeman collared me and said, "You are not going to get away to-night;" I asked him what he meant; he said, "You must come to the station," and he swore he saw me pick the gentleman's pocket, which I know nothing about.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1401. GEORGE NORRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 5 3/4 lbs. weight of mutton, value 4 s., the goods of John Membry.
JOHN MEMBRY . I am a butcher, and live at Royal-hill, Greenwich. About nine o'clock on Saturday evening, the 12th of March, the prisoner came into my shop and told me to send Mr. Baldwin a small leg of mutton—Mr. Baldwin was a customer of mine—I sent my boy with a leg of mutton to Mr. Baldwin's—the constable came back with a leg of mutton on the Sunday—it was then cut in two, and part of it cooked—I could not swear to it.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are you certain about the prisoner? A. Yes, confident—I was not told by the policeman that the Magistrate ordered I should prosecute—he did not tell me himself that I must.
THOMAS GEORGE PHYPERS . I was sent with a leg of mutton to Mr. Baldwin's—as I was going along Upper George-street the prisoner stopped me and said, "Are you going to Mr. Baldwin's?"—I said, "Yes"—he said, "Stop a minute, Mr. Baldwin is gone out"—I said, "Very well, I will take this leg down to Mr. Lloyd, and call as I come back"—he met me again—he said, "Mr. Baldwin is not at home yet, I don't like to pay you for it till he sees it"—I said, "Very well, I will take it home"—he said, "Stop a minute," he took it out of my basket and went towards the door, and I went home.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen him before? A. Yes, he is a French polisher—I did not say it was so dark I could not see his face, and that I knew him by the smell of the paint—I am certain of that—I let him take the leg of mutton out of my basket.
JOHN MEMBRY re-examined. I believed the prisoner's story to be true, knowing him to be a neighbour of Mr. Baldwin's I thought he might have called to order it—the boy had no authority from me to give it to the prisoner—I should not have delivered it to him myself. REUBEN BALDWIN. I never got this mutton. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined One Month.

1403. JOHN SMITH was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JACOB DA VIES ( police-constable R 269.) On the 1st of April I was called into the back-yard of the Old Chapel public-house—I went and found three men stripped fighting—in consequence of that I took the prisoner into custody—he drew his knife from round him, and said he had used that knife in the defence of his country many years, and he would use it again—he made a stab at me—it did not come very near to me, the door not being shut, but my stepping back shut the door—he stabbed at me a second time, but did not cut me—he made three stabs at me—I believe he attempted to hit me with it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. He was a seaman on board the Gorgon? A. Yes, and had been in the war in Syria—seamen wear their
knives round their waist—it was open—he was not sober—I had laid hold of him and brought him into the parlour—he did not touch my clothes.
ROBERT SAUNDERS . I am a policeman. I was present—I have heard what Davies has said—it is true—I should say the knife was half an inch from his body.
Cross-examined. Q. He did not touch you? A. He did not attempt it. GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18420404-1404]
1404. RICHARD BATES was indicted for that he, on the 12th of March, knowingly and feloniously did send to William Henry Carlin a certain letter accusing him of having committed b—y, with intent to extort a 10 l. Bank of England note, his property.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be for threatening to accuse him, with the like intent.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HENRY CARLIN . I am a tobacconist, living at No. 25, Ludgate-street, City. On the 12th of March I received a letter, which I now produce, by post—it came by the first delivery in the morning—I immediately went to the Justice sitting at Guildhall, and got the assistance of Roe the officer—by his advice I addressed a letter to Mr. Richard, and put it in the post-office—I went the same afternoon, with Roe, to No. 55, High-street, Borough—I found Greenland the shopman there—I waited there with Roe about two hours, then left directions with Greenland—on the 14th the prisoner was brought to my shop in custody—I knew nothing of him whatever—I had never seen him in my life to my knowledge—I went to the station, and entered the charge.
JOHN GREENLAND . At the time in question I was shopman to the post-master, at No. 55, High-street, Borough—the prosecutor and Roe came there on the 12th of March—they gave me directions what to do, and on the Utah of March the prisoner came to the post-office, and asked for a letter in the name of Richard—I told him he could not have it, as it was in master's possession, that master had the care of those letters, and asked him to wait—I sent out for a policeman, but the boy could not find one, and the prisoner left before one arrived—I followed him to Tooley-street, Borougn, and there gave him into custody—he never had the letter—the letter produced by the prosecutor has been posted at our office in High-street.
Prisoner. I asked him for a letter for Richard Bates. Witness He did not—he said, "Is there a letter left here for the name of Richard?" I said, "There is"—I am sure it was Richard, and not Richard Bates, that he mentioned.
MICHAEL TUNNICLIFFE . I am a clerk in the Register-office, Somerset House. I have known the prisoner about thirty years—I have seen him write—I was a townsman of his, and have received letters from him for relief, and answered them—I received a letter from him the beginning of March—Roe came to me about the 14th or 15th of March, and at that time there was a letter lying on my table which I had from the prisoner that morning—I am sure the letter produced is his hand-writing—I should say it was his—it is some years since I saw him write—I had received several letters from him previous to the 14th of March, and relieved him when he
has called upon me in consequence of those letters—I cannot say whether he referred to the letters when he called, but he called several times—I said it was not in my power to do any thing more, and not to call again, and after that he wrote to me—he never wrote to me but to request relief. ( Letter read)—"London, March 11th, 1842. Mr. Carlin—Sir, I am sorry to have in my power to inform you of a circumstance which take place between you and a gentleman, it is of such a nature that was it publicly known, would not only be against you, but indeed your shop would be deserted—this gentleman states he has had connexions with in a most unaturely way—I am an entire stranger to you—should you wish to put an end it such a report being spread abroad, address your letters to 55, High-street, Boro', enclosing a ten pound note, and you shall be in possession of all, and a stop put to any further to do about—do not fail sending by return of post, addressed to Mr. Richard."
WILLIAM BURROW ( police-constable, M 116.) I took the prisoner into custody by desire of Mr. Greenland.
Prisoner's Defence. At the beginning of the week I had written to the west end respecting a situation, desiring they would give me an answer to 55, High-street, Borough; on the Monday night in question I called at the post-office to ask for a letter directed to Richard Bates, the moment I mentioned the name the person said he thought his master had a letter for that name; but at the time, the witness was engaged with a person taking letters from him, and he wanted 5 s. worth of letters perhaps; I was there some time; I said I was going further and would call again as I returned; I walked on where I had to go, intending to call on my return, but I am certainly innocent of the letter; I asked for the name of Richard Bates, he never asked my name; I never received any letter nor asked if that letter was intended for me, nor was it given or shown to me.
JOHN GREENLAND re-examined. There was no letter at the post-office directed to Richard Bates.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18420404-1405]
1405. WILLIAM BAKER was indicted for embezzlement.
Before Mr. Recorder.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT STAINTON DIXON . I am a coal-merchant, and live in the Belvidere-road. The prisoner was my clerk—it was his duty to receive money from customers—if he received on the 15th of November, 8 l. 1 s. from Mr. Hodson, or 2 l. 4 s. from Mr. Ison, on the 4th of November, or the same sum on the 4th of February, he has not accounted for them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was he always to account to you? A. Howard, the cashier, generally received the money—I have had 14 l. and a bill for 45 l. from the prisoner's friends.
COURT. Q. How is that? A. I have been from time to time solicited by his mother and other friends to let him off—I would not hear anything of the kind for a long time, but a friend of mine had asked me several times to do it, and said they could raise me 60 l.—my loss is nearly 500 l.—I said "What is 60 l. to my loss, and I understand his friends are poor, and I do not feel satisfied with taking money from poor people," but ultimately they persuaded my wife to get me to settle it—I said, "Well, as 60 l. is offered me I may as well take it, that he may be under some obligation to his friends"—I said I would apply for his release, and they came one night and gave me 14 l. and agreed on my applying for his release to
give me 45 l. more—I applied to Mr. Cottingham next day, who said it was too bad a case, he could not allow it to pass—I told Mr. Marriage, the prisoner's friend, as the thing had taken a different turn, that I had done what I could—I considered I was entitled to retain the money, but said, "I do not wish to do so, you may as well take the money and the undertaking for the 45 l. back"—I never had the prisoner's acceptance for any money—I said when I offered him the money, "Now I don't undertake if you make use of this settlement in Court. I do not engage to give you the 14 l. and the undertaking after the trial," but however he would not take it.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Is this letter in the prisoner's handwriting? A. Yes—it was after I received the letter that this mony transaction took place. ( This letter was dated from Horsemonger-lane gaol, expressing the prisoner's deep regret at his ingratitude to so kind a master, earnestly soliciting pardon, and expressing a hope that part of the prosecutor's loss might be made up by his friends.)
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you receive a bill of exchange for 45 l. from Marriage, which you was to give up, on payment of 30 l. in cash? A. I received the undertaking—a bill for 15 l. was never offered me—I did not receive the prisoner's note of hand for 350 l.—this it my handwriting—I signed it—( read)—"I hereby acknowledge to hold a bill of exchange, accepted by you for 45 l. in my favour, which I agree to give op to you, on payment of 30 l. cash, and Mr. John Mason's acceptance for 15 l., and William Baker, my late clerk's note of hand for 350 l."—I see I engage to give up the 45 l. on the bill and 350 l. acceptance being given to me, but I have never had them—if I had got them I certainly intended to release him, but the Magistrate would not allow it.
JOHN HODSON . On the 15th of November I paid the prisoner 8 l. 1 s., for Mr. Dixon.
JOHN HOWARD . I am cashier to Mr. Dixon—I have the prisoner's book here of sums received—there is no entry of 8 l. 1 s. on the 15th of November, nor after that, as received from Hodson—he has not accounted to me for it in any way—he has not accounted for 2 l. 4 s. from Mr. Ison, on the 4th of December, nor 2 l. 4 s. on the 4th of February from him—I have looked through the books, there is no account of it—he has accounted for many other sums from other people since the 15th of November.
WILLIAM ISON . I paid the prisoner, on the 4th of December, 2 l, 4 s., and on the 4th of February 2 l. 4 s.—here are his receipts for these sums, given by himself.
MR. PHILLIPS to MR. DIXON. Q. Did you ask the prisoner how his deficiencies originated, and did not he say it was in his having 50 l. of yours, which he had lost in an omnibus, having placed it by his side, he having taken rather too much drink? A. He said 40 l., but he gave me two versions of it, once saying he had drunk too much, without saying he had lost it in an omnibus, or where—this was on the evening I found out his deficiencies.
(—Marriage, William Fuller, dressing-casemaker, Brownlow-street, Holborn; and John Coles, corn-dealer, Brewer-street, Somers-town; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Justice Coleridge.
[trialAccount t18420404-1406]
1406. JOHN RICE , JOHN LODAN , and MATTHEW GRAVESTOCK , were indicted for breaking and entering the warehouse of Emanuel Silva and another, on the 28th of March, at St. Olave, Southwark and stealing therein, 7 gallons of oil, value 1 l. 1 s., their goods; to which
RICE pleaded GUILTY . Aged 46.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS BOULTON . I am a policeman. I was on duty in Morgan's-lane, Tooley-street, on Monday, the 27th of March, from nine o'clock at night till six o'clock next morning—I know the prosecutors' warehouses, they were all safe in the morning, when I went off duty at six o'clock—I returned again at nine o'clock the same evening—it is my habit to examine the fastenings every night, as I come on my beat—I did so to the prosecutors' warehouse, a little after nine o'clock, and found this padlock hanging on the staple of the door, with the bottom upwards—it was unlocked, but hanging as if it was locked—I tried it, and found it came open—the outside iron bar was up as usual, and the lock on the staple—I looked about a little, and saw Rice, standing about twenty yards off—he walked past me, close by me, and when he had passed me, six or seven yards, he turned back again, and examined a bill that was on the door, and as he walked away from the bill, he asked me if the door was unlocked—I said, "Why?"—he said, "For nothing"—I asked him if he belonged to the premises—he said, "Oh no," and walked about eight or ten yards, and turned up against the wall, as if for a necessary purpose—when he left, I went and examined the place where he had been standing, and found it perfectly dry—he walked opposite the public-house adjoining the ware-house, and went in at the front-door—after speaking to the watchman I went back to the warehouse-door, which I had left about four minutes to call the watchman—on my return, I found the padlock had been turned up the other way, and locked in its usual way—I saw Rice again in about half-an-hour, coming down Morgan's-lane, towards the warehouse—I said something to him—I did not say it would be better or worse if he said any thing—I said I should take him to the station, on suspicion of doing something wrong—he said, "Very well," he would go along with me, which he did quietly—I asked if he had got any keys about him, he said, "No"—I said, "I dare say you have no objection to walk down to the warehouse-door," which he did, and the watchman asked him if he had any keys about him—he said "No" again—the watchman said, "If you have got the key, you had better give it up"—he said he had not got it—I took him to the station—on the road to the station I saw him try to pass something to a boy who he met—I looked to see what it was in his hand, and saw it was a key, with this wood to it, marked D 1—I took him to the station, took the key, went back to the warehouse, and found it would unlock the lock—I unlocked the door with it, took the padlock off, turned the iron bolt down, and found the door fastened inside—I could not get in—we got a crow-bar put in between the two doors, (it is a double door,) and wrenched the bolt back—it took nearly an hour to do that—when we got inside we found the prisoners Lodan and Gravestock inside, standing behind a crane—I told them to come to the station, and asked if there was any body besides them inside—they said there was not—I locked the door, took the key with me, and took them to the station—they said it was a bad job—I came back, and found four tin cans in the warehouse, holding about four gallons each—two of them were full of oil—I found a great
many barrels of oil in the warehouse, and the remains of a candle which had been burning—my brother constable picked it up from behind where the prisoners had been standing—I was with Berry when he examined the barrels—I found one side of one barrel was wet and the other dry, as if the oil had been running down from a spile-hole—here are two phials, containing a sample of oil, one from each of the full tin cans—and Berry has a sample from two different barrels, both of which appeared to be wet.
Lodan. When I came up the lane the door was open.
COURT. Q. What time passed from the time you found the door unfastened till you found it locked again? Witness. Three or four minutes—I saw nobody there but Rice—I could not see whether there was a candle burning inside while the door was shut—I left a watchman there while I went to the station, and during the half-hour while I was away looking for Rice—the number of the warehouse is D No. 1, the same as is on the key—there is no door into the warehouse but this one.
JAMES JONES . I am foreman to Messrs. Emanuel and Thomas Silva, wharfingers. I know the warehouse D No. 1—there were thirty-five casks of oil in it, some was brown seal, and some pale seal oil—on Wednesday, the 23rd of March, I had been at the warehouse—I then had this key with the label now produced—when I left I locked the door, and hung the key in the counting-house, in its proper place—I had no occasion to go to it after that before the discovery, nor had anybody else to my knowledge—if the key had remained in its proper place, it should have been hanging up in the counting-house—Rice was a weekly servant of Messrs. Silva—I did not know him before he came into their service—I have known Lodan many years—he was occasionally employed by master, but seldom—he knew all our warehouses very well—I do not know Gravestock—we keep candles in the counting-house—the candle which was found did not belong to us—we do not use candles in the warehouse, but oil.
COURT. Q. Was Rice in your master's employment at this time? A Yes, but not Lodan—Rice would have to come to the counting-house in the course of his employment, and would take the key, if he wanted it, as I should myself—there was an old bolt to the warehouse-door inside, but I did not know it till this happened.
JAMES BERRY . I am cellarman to Pilcher and Co., oil-merchants, in Morgan's-lane. I have dipped two barrels of oil at the warehouse of the prosecutors, D No. 1, to see what was gone—they appeared to be deficient—I took a sample from the two barrels, which I produce—I dipped several—they differed in colour—these two were what I call straw-oil, and corresponded with that found in the cans—the ground was wet with oil—here are two samples from the cans—in my judgment they are the same quality as these samples taken from the cans—they are all straw-oil, but one barrel is paler than the other—the oil in. one barrel corresponds with one can, and the other with the other.
JOSHUA BROWNELL . I am clerk to the prosecutors. I know nothing of Gravestock—I was not present when the barrels were gauged, but I have the gauge from the sworn gauger in the City—I was present when the barrels were first landed, and then saw him gauge them—there was a much larger cargo—I know the two barrels in question were quite full in February, and made complete—from the examination I have made since, there is a deficiency of seven or eight gallons, which would be about what the two cans
would hold—the total deficiency in the thirty-five casks is eighty gallons—Lodan was occasionally employed by my master—on this day, or the night of the 28th, Rice had not the slightest business with the key—he had been employed there six or seven years regularly.
COURT. Q. YOU say the casks were filled up in February? A. Yes—none had been sold from them—it is usual, when a quantity of oil is purchased, for the cooper to come and fill up the casks, as there is a wastage—there could not be a waste of this quantity since February, as the casks were in good condition—it was impossible there could be much leakage—there might be a very trifling one.
Lodan. The two four-gallon cans were quite full; the premises were not broken open. Witness. The four-gallon cans did not belong to my employer, and were not there on the 23rd.
JAMES JONES re-examined. I saw these two gimlets found in the cellar—they had no business there—I saw them compared with the holes in the casks—I, did not see any which agreed with the gimlets—I saw no fresh holes made there.
COURT. Q. Did you see any hole out of which the oil could be drawn? A. I did not—the spile-holes did not appear enlarged—the place where the policeman said oil appeared to have been running, might have been from the rolling of the casks.
THOMAS BOULTON re-examined. The wet was from the spile-hole—it appeared to have run from the spile-hole and from the bung-hole—both are on the top of the cask—they lay lengthways—the cask had been rolled over on its side a little, and then the oil would run out—there was a little tow in the spile-hole, which appeared very wet—the holes were about the size which this gimlet would bore—the casks seemed wet down the side, as if the oil had ran from it—one side was perfectly dry—if it had been rolled right over, both sides would have been wet.
JAMES BERRY re-examined. Q. If they had bored at one end of the cask with the gimlet, there was air enough to let the oil run out? A. Yes—there was a vent to the cask.
(Dennis Ragan, a shoemaker, of Snow's-fields, Bermondsey; James Macfeeney, undertaker, of Snow's-fields; James Doughty, a shoemaker, of No. 4, Union-yard, Tooley-street; and—Scott, glass-cutter, of Holland-street; gave the prisoner Rice a good character. Thomas Barker and William Gregory gave Lodan a good character.)
RICE— GUILTY. Aged 46.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
LODAN— GUILTY . Aged 46.— Confined Twelve Months.
GRAVESTOCK— GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Eight Months.
Before Mr. Justice Erskine.
[trialAccount t18420404-1407]
1407. JOHN JONES was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Edward Masters Atchison, on the 4th of April. at St. Mary, Newington, and stealing therein 1 coat, value 2 l. 10 s., 1 waistcoat, value 10 s., and 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l., the goods of Thomas Pollux Dehay.
THOMAS POLLUX DEHAY . I live at No. 18, Bedford-terrace, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington. On the 4th of April, about ten minutes before ten o'clock in the morning, I left my house—I only have the back-room ground-floor—the door opens into the passage, leading to the street
—Mr. Edward Masters Atchison, the landlord, lives in the house—he occupies the room below—the floor is let to lodgers—there is another floor above that, which the landlord occupies—I returned about two o'clock in the morning—I had left a coat and waistcoat on the sideboard in the room—I did not lock my door myself—I left it open—when I got up in the morning I missed my coat, waistcoat, and trowsers—I saw them in the possession of the officer.
ELIZABETH MAYCOCK . I live at No. 15, Bedford-terrace, Newington—I had charge of the keys of No. 18—I went there on the 4th of April, at four o'clock in the afternoon, to call Mr. Atchison out, and found nobody but him at home—he is a compositor on the "Times"—he left his house about ten minutes after four o'clock, and came into my house and took tea, leaving nobody in the house—he locked the door after him, and I went there again about a quarter to seven o'clock as the keys were left in my possession—I went into every room and found everything correct—I went into the prosecutor's room—I did not notice anything there particular—when I came out I locked the door, the windows were all fastened, and I took the key of the door home with me—in consequence of information I went to the house again in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour—I found the street door locked as I had left it, but the back door wide open—it opens into the garden—the door was bolted top and bottom when I left—it must have been opened from within—I found Mr. Dehay's room door wide open, but missed nothing myself—I had left his room door locked, with the key outside.
ANN CHURCH . I live at No. 8, Victoria-terrace, exactly opposite Mr. Atchison's house. On the 4th of April, about one o'clock, I saw three very suspicious characters about Mr. Atchison's house; and again about half-past seven o'clock in the evening I saw a man knock repeatedly at the front-door—nobody answered it—and between seven and eight o'clock I saw a man, I cannot say whether it was the same, enter the house and shut the door after him—I cannot say whether he did anything before entering the house—I saw nobody else about the house at that time—I immediately went to give information.
THOMAS NICHOLSON RULER . I live at No. 2, West-terrace, St. George's-row—I know Mr. Atchison's house—there is a back yard to my house which is about fifty feet from Mr. Atchison's back yard—it crosses a ditch and a wide garden—on the evening of the 4th of April I saw the prisoner in my yard, on my back premises—I went up to him and asked what he did on the premises—he said he was sent there—I asked him who sent him—he said he was sent through our house—I asked him what that was on his arm, he had some wearing apparel on his arm—he said it was his own property—I took him into the house and my father sent for a policeman, who came and took the property and some skeleton keys from him—he took him into custody—he had a coat and a pair of trowsers on his arm—I went back into the yard and saw footsteps in a direction from the back wall of Mr. Atchison's, across the garden ground to our premises—they were recently made—I did not find any other articles myself.
JOHN COOK ( police-constable N 8.) On the evening of the 4th of April I was sent for to Ruler's house, and took the prisoner into custody—I found two skeleton keys up his coat sleeve, and a tin box with lucifers, and a candle in it—Ruler said there was a coat and trowsers belonging to him—the prisoner was outside the door when I took him, and I saw the
things lying down in the passage—I left them in Ruler's care, and took the prisoner to the station—I afterwards examined Mr. Atchison's premises—I went in at the front door, got a candle, and went out at the back, and found this waistcoat in the back yard—I have had it ever since—I traced footsteps from Mr. Atchison's back yard wall, across the garden into Ruler's premises—I tried the skeleton keys to the lock of the front door next morning, and the largest opened it.
Prisoner. When you took me in charge you took me outside the door; the clothes were in the house; the landlord brought them to the station, and said I had stolen them—I was taken with the two keys in my hand, which I had just picked up. Witness. The keys were up his sleeve—I had a hard matter to get them out.
THOMAS NICHOLSON RULER re-examined. When I met the prisoner in the yard, he had the trowsers and coat over his arm—before the policeman came, the coat and trowsers had dropped off his arm in the scuffle, and fell in the passage—I afterwards saw the policeman take possession of the same coat and waistcoat—as soon as my father said, "Send for a policeman," the prisoner scuffled to get away.
THOMAS POLLUX DEHAY re-examined. This coat, waistcoat, and trowsers are mine—I know the coat by its general appearance—there is no mark on it—I had it about three months—I know the trowsers and waistcoat in the same way—I have no mark on them—I believe them to be mine, and what I left in the house that day.
Prisoner's Defence. When the policeman took me, he took me outside the door, with two keys in my hand; the policeman brought the clothes to the station; he went back to the garden to see if he could see anything more; the man went first to trace footmarks, and he saw no waistcoat there; the policeman goes afterwards and finds one; they said at the station that I jumped over a wall fifteen feet high; is it likely I could do that and over a ditch? the keys I picked up by chance, and had them in my hand; they wanted to take them from me, but I would not give them to anybody but the policeman; they dragged me along and almost strangled me; when the policeman came I said I would go with him—I went to ask for the name of Jones, who I was directed to.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Eighteen Months.
Before Mr. Justice Erskine.

1408. CHARLOTTE WEATHERHEAD was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, a letter containing a purse, value 2 s., the property of her Majesty's Postmaster-General, she being a person employed under the Post Office.—5 other COUNTS varying the manner of laying the charge. MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL, with MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and SHEPHERD
conducted the Prosecution.
MARY BIGGS . I live with my father in Walcot-place, Lambeth. On the 12th of February, I wrote a letter to Miss Cohen, and enclosed in it a black and yellow silk purse, with a steel clasp—it was wrapped in blue paper—I sealed the letter with the purse in it, and gave it to my sister or the servant, I cannot tell which, to put into the post—( looking at a purse produced by Matthew Peek)—this the purse I enclosed, to the best of my belief—I worked it myself—my sister bought the steel-clasp—she did no give it to me—she sewed it on the purse—the blue paper it is now in is similar to that I wrapped it in—to the best of my belief this is the Purse
I worked and sent to Miss Cohen—I directed the letter to Miss Cohen, at Mr. Hawker's, High-street, Kensington.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. DO yon occasionally wear glasses? A. No, I am not at all short-sighted—I believe this to be the purse.
Q. But do you mean to swear positively to it? A. Two purses may be made alike—I have no doubt of it—I swear to it as far as I possibly can—I have no doubt of it—I have sworn to it, but no further than to the best of my belief—I should think it is the same—I put no mark on it, and another person might make the same kind of purse—I am almost positive—I have no doubt of it—I do not swear positively.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. YOU say you are almost positive it is your purse? A Yes, but I should be afraid to swear positively—I observe no difference between this and the purse I made—I have no doubt about it being mine.
SARAH ANN BIGGS . I am the witness's sister. I remember, on the 12th of February, her putting a purse into a letter directed to Miss Cohen—I bought the clasp of it in Westminster road, and my mother sewed it on—my sister worked the purse—I saw the same purse put into the letter—I believe the purse produced to be the same—I have no doubt of it, and I believe the clasp to be the one I bought—I cannot swear to my mother's stitching—the purse, in its colour and every particular, has the appearance of the one my sister made—after it was put into the letter I took it down stairs myself from my sister, and delivered it to Susan Walker, the servant, to carry to the post—the purse was enclosed in a piece of blue paper, just like the blue paper produced—the post-office is immediately opposite our house, at No. 5, East-place—Mrs. Weatherhead, the prisoner's mother, keeps it—the prisoner lives there with her—I know her to be the daughter, and have seen her there, but it is a long time ago.
Cross-examined. Q. When were yon at the post-office kept by Mrs. Weatherhead? A. I do not know—some time last year—I have been there two or three times I believe—I only know Mrs. Weatherhead by going into the shop—I was not acquainted with the prisoner—I mean to state positively this is the purse my sister worked—I swear it—I am sure of it—I know it by the look of it—these purses are worked from patterns—this was worked from a pattern—I suppose the patterns are to be bought—I do not know—there is no mark on the clasp but these little cuts on it—there are many hundreds of them—it is similar to the one I saw—that is the reason 1 swear to it.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you see your sister when she made this purse have any pattern by her? A. Oh, no, she had no pattern by her—I do not know that she worked it from a pattern—she has worked one of the same pattern before—seeing the clasp and purse together I believe it to be the same.
MARY BIGGS . I am the witness's mother. I remember my daughter making a purse—I put the clasp to it—I think there cannot be a doubt that this is the same purse.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean by that to swear it is the same? A Why, to swear positively, one is almost afraid—another might be like it—my daughter has made several of the same pattern—I cannot say whether she has made half a dozen.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. DO you mean the same colours as well
as the same shape and form? A. I do not think she has made one of these colours, but I am not certain she has made the same pattern.
SUSAN WALKER . I am servant to Mrs. Biggs. I remember about six weeks ago receiving a letter from Miss Sarah Ann Biggs to take to the post-office—it was on a Saturday—I do not remember the day of the month—there was something in it—I do not know what—I took it to the post-office kept by Mrs. Weatherhead, nearly opposite—I gave it to the youngest daughter, the prisoner's sister—I asked her how much the postage was—she said 2 d and I gave her 2 d.—a few days after Miss Cohen called at mistress's, and in consequence of what passed I went over to the post-office and saw the prisoner—I said the lady had come to our house, and she had not had the letter which I brought over two or three days ago, could she tell mistress how to trace it out—she said no, she could not tell her, there was no way of tracing it unless the post-office let them know—I said there was a purse in it, and mistress was very vexed about it—she said if that was ail, it was of no consequence, there were many worse losses than that.
Cross-examined. Q. At what time of day did you put the letter in? A. About four or half-past four o'clock—I am sure it was between four and five.
CATHERINE COHEN . I live at Mr. Hawker's, High-street, Kensington. I did not, in February, receive a letter from Miss Biggs, containing a silk purse—I did not know of her intention of sending one—I became apprised of it when I called on her on Wednesday or Thursday, about the 16th or 17th—I am not quite sure—I did not receive any letter from her in February.
MATTHEW PEEK . I am a constable employed at the post-office. I went to the receiving-house in East-place, Lambeth on Friday, the 1st of April, with Mr. Bell and Mr. Peacock—the name of Weatherhead was over the door—I was never there before—I had a search warrant, and searched the house—the prisoner was not at home when I commenced—I found in the back-parlour this paper box on the sideboard by the side of the fire, and found this purse in it, wrapped in this blue paper—I am quite certain it is the same—here are my initials and the date on the paper, in my own writing—I laid the purse and box on the table, out of the box—I believe Mr. Bell put it into the box—the prisoner came in about five minutes after—Mr. Peacock, Mr. Bell, the prisoner's mother and sister were present—Mr. Peacock held the purse in his hand and said, "Who does this belong to?" or "Whose is this?"—the prisoner said, "It is mine"—the question was asked, "How long have you had it?"—she said about three months—"Where did you get it from?"—she said, "I had it given to me by a friend"—"Where did he get it?"—she said somewhere in the Strand—he bought it somewhere in the Strand—she was asked where about—she immediately turned round, addressing her sister and said, "It is yours"—the sister said it was not—another question was put, I do not recollect what, but she made no answer—I finished my search, came down stairs, and the prisoner said she bought the purse at the Bazaar—I asked which, and she said she believed it was the one in Soho, she had been to them both—I was ordered to take her into custody, and took her to the post-office. and then to the Police Court, Bow-street—on our way there she said, "If I was to tell who took the purse, what would they be done to?"—
I said I could not answer the question—I found three other purses, two op stairs and one in a reticule or bag, which she brought in with her.
Cross-examined. Q. Produce the three? A. ( Does so)—this is not all I brought from the house—here are some cambric pocket handkerchiefs, and two silk bags—I brought the handkerchiefs away, as my inquiry was for a cambric handkerchief.
Q. On any of the purses you have last produced, have any of the rings been altered? A. None what ever, to my knowledge—none that I have had in my possession have—I have had these two brown owes—these two red ones have been in Mr. Bell's possession—I saw them when found—this one is the subject of another charge—these are the rings which were on it when found, to the best of my belief—it has been in Mr. Bell's possession—I will swear they were exactly like these—I am quite certain these on one purse were not on the other—they have not been changed to my knowledge, and they have all the appearance of the same rings as when I found them—I perfectly recollect finding them, and the other rings were rather broader—I have not been to the Bazaar to see if such purses are sold there—we had been about ten minutes in the house when the prisoner came in, and were searching then—it was stated to the mother that I was an officer—I do not know whether it was named to the prisoner, but she saw me searching—the sister never told me the box was hers, I am quite sure.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. Did you put a mark on this purse when you found it? A. Yes, an ink-mark just at the tassel, on the silk—the rings have not been changed—I think I found about a dozen cambric handkerchiefs, altogether—they are three or four different patterns—one or two are marked—I have not examined to see what the marks are—I was directed to look for cambric handkerchiefs by Mr. Bell.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Are you judge enough to speak to their being cambric handkerchiefs? A. I am not.
SAMUEL BELL . I am a president of the Twopenny Post-office. I know the receiving-house in East-place, Lambeth—it is conducted by Mrs. Weatherhead—I saw the prisoner there for the first time on the 1st of April—a letter posted at that office on the 12th of February, between four and five o'clock, would arrive at the office in London the same evening, and be forwarded to Kensington for delivery next morning—on the 1st of April, in consequence of directions, I went with Peek to the prisoner's mother's—we had some conversation with the mother, and while the officer was searching, the prisoner came in—I saw Peek find some property, and among other things, this box with the lid on—this purse was in it, wrapped up in this blue paper—the prisoner was not there when it was found—the mother and sister were—the prisoner was present when Mr. Peacock took up the purse, and said, "To whom does this purse belong?"—the prisoner said, "It is mine"—she was asked how long she had had it, and answered, about three months—"Where did you get it?" said Mr. Peacock—She said, "I bought it"—he said, "Where did you buy it?"—she said, "A friend gave it to me, and he bought it some where in the Strand"—I am not certain which she called her friend, he or she—on being asked who gave it to her, she said, "It is not mine, it was given to my sister"—her sister was present.
Q. What words did she use? A. She said to her sister, "You know it is yours"—the sister replied, "It is not mine, I know nothing about it"
—the prisoner was again asked who gave it to her—she made no answer—I afterwards went to Mrs. Biggs's house with the purse, returned, and the prisoner was told the purse had been identified as one sent in a letter, and she must accompany the officer to the Post-office.
Cross-examined. Q. If a letter was put in at Lambeth between five and six o'clock, what time would it find its way to the General Post-office London? A. Between six and seven o'clock, or probably before six—Peek, the officer, found these other purses—I was not present when one was found, but was when this other was found by Peek, with his own hand—I saw him find this one in the back room on the first floor—I was not present when this other was found—Mr. Peacock, Peek, and the women were present at the house, nobody else in that room—I do not know whether there is a family living there besides Mrs. Weatherhead's—I did not see any other person—I did not see the paper box till Peek produced it—the prisoner was not present, but the sister was—I think the question was asked as to whose box it was, and either the sister or mother answered, "It belongs to us all"—the prisoner was then absent—I cannot recollect that the sister said it was her box—I will not say she did not—I do not remember hearing her say so—I understood it to be common property among the whole of them.
ROBERT WILLIAM PEACOCK . I am assistant to my brother, the solicitor to the Post-office. I was present on this occasion—inquiry was made of the prisoner whose purse that was—she answered, "It is mine"—I asked how long she had had it—she said, "About three months"—I said, "Where did you get it?"—she at first said, "I bought it"—I said, "Where?"—she answered, "It was given to me by a friend, who purchased it some where in the Strand"—I said, "At what place, what is the name of your friend?"—she turned round to her sister who was sitting by the side of her, and said, "Why, it is yours"—the sister replied, "It is not mine, I know nothing about it"—I then asked the prisoner again, "Who gave it you?"—she made no reply.
HERBERT PRITCHARD . I am a letter-carrier, in the Twopenny Post-office, and collect the letters from Mrs. Weatherhead's office twice a day—the prisoner generally made up the collection of letters for me—she has been in the habit of doing so, I should say for the last twelve months—she occasionally took the letters out, she stamped them, and tied them up—I should say I have seen her receive letters in the office, and receive the postage.
MRS. LOUISA PEPPERCORN ( looking at one of the purses produced.) I know this purse—I made it myself—I put the tassels on myself, and the slides—this slide is one that was on it—the other which was on it was like it—( the other slide had been mislaid in Court.)
Q. Look at this other purse, do the slides on that belong to your purse? A. Never, as far as I know—I am quite certain the first purse I saw is mine—I inclosed it in an envelope, and took it to Mrs. Weatherhead's post-office, East-place.
Cross-examined. Q. Is there any thing about the purse you identify, except its general appearance, by which you swear to it? A. Only being exactly the same in every respect as I made one—it was quite new when I parted with it—it does not appear now to have been washed—it has been worn—not very much—it is stained—it is of the commonest pattern.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. You do not recognize any part of your
own work there at all? A. No—I made my purse round, which is not the general way of making a purse, but there have been purses made so—I have not the least doubt of it being mine.
AMELIA WEATHERHEAD ( the prisoner's sister.) This is not my purse in this blue paper—I know nothing about it.
Cross-examined. Q. Whose paper box is this? A. Mine—I told Mr. Peacock so, but not the officer, when they searched the house.
MR. CLABKSON called
ELIZABETH REDGRAVE . I live at No. 6, Walcot-square, Lambeth. My brother is an attorney—I live with him—I know the prisoner—I have been on terms of intimacy with her for a year and a half, or better—and have visited her for that time—she came to my house on the 12th of February, Saturday, about three o'clock in the afternoon—she came to take tea and supper—I have referde to a book to ascertain that it was the 12th of February—she did not leave my society till she left for good that night, which was at ten o'clock—we went out shopping together—she did not go home between three and ten o'clock—she returned, and took tea and supper with me—I have seen this purse two or three times before—( the one identified by Mrs. Peppercorn)—I have not seen it since it was finished—I saw the prisoner knitting it in the month of January—if it it not the same, it is exactly like it—she has brought it to my house—she has worked at it there—to the best of my belief this is the material on which she was working—I know the difference between working it round and working it the other way.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. Do you know the meaning of a purse being worked round? A. No, unless it is what I have seen, worked round in this way—I saw her at work on this in January—I never saw the clasps, or tassels, or rings—I have seen it two or three times—I know nothing of this other purse—I did not see the tassels or rings put on that—I have examined the first one, and she complained of the silk being fine at the time—she came to my house at three o'clock, on the 12th of February—I remember the day very well—I find a memorandum in my book—I am come forward to speak the truth—I always make a memorandum of every thing I purchase—on that day I made several purchases—I can name the articles—my attention was drawn to the fact on the evening of the day the prisoner was taken into custody—I was not there when she was taken—I heard of it in the evening of the same day—I do not know that I named to any body particularly that I could prove she was from the post-office from three to ten—the attorney applied to me to give evidence yesterday evening—I never communicated with him before that—I told him what evidence I should be able to give, but never before last night—I had named it to my brother, being with him, that I had put it down—I referred to my memorandum-book directly I heard of it—I know Mrs. Weatherhead, but I am more acquainted with Miss Weatherhead—I visited there frequently—I have not visited there since the 1st of April—I have seen Mrs. Weatherhead since then—I mentioned to her that her daughter was with me that day—I should think the prisoner is about twenty-one years of age—I had called in the morning, and gave her an invitation to go shopping—I saw her and her mother and sister, but they did not hear me that I know of—I did not hear the prisoner inform her mother that she was going—I made no application to the mother at all—the prisoner came about three o'clock—that was after my dinner—we did not drink tea before we went out—we went up the Blackfriars-road to
several shops to White and Green well's, and to Ward's, and to Howell's, in Blackfriars-road, and bought things at all of them—I have been at all of the shops several times, but am not known at any of them—I bought things at several other shops, which I paid for—I had bills at the time, but I never keep them—we went to the toy-shop in the Blackfriar's-road, and called at a hair-dresser's in the Blackfriars-road, but I do not know the name—I called to know the price of some combs—I went to no shop at which I was known—we got back between five and six o'clock—we waited tea for my brother who returns home at six, and drank tea a little after six—we did not go out shopping again—we did not go out afterwards—the prisoner never left my presence till she went home at ten—our house is not five minutes' walk from her mother's—I make my memorandums in my house-keeping book—it is a large square diary—I have not brought it here—I should think it was inconvenient to carry—I should say it is as heavy as that book on that table—both my brothers were there—we have no servant—we have a charwoman occasionally—she was not there that day—I got the tea ready—one of my brothers comes home to tea at six—the other comes home at eight.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You made several purchases? A. Yes, the prisoner was with me all the time—when she was taken up last Friday, I mentioned this circumstance to my brother.
GEORGE REDGRAVE . I am in the office of Caman and Hayes, solicitors, Chancery-lane, and live with my sister, in Walcot-square. I generally go home from the office-duty at half-past five o'clock, and get home at six or a few minutes after—I remember coming home on the 12th of February—I cannot be positive to the time to a minute or two—I found the prisoner with my sister—I had known her before for nearly two years—our family and hers had been intimate—she took tea and supper with us—she returned home as near ten as possible—it might be a short time after—I have seen the prisoner working on a purse of the same colour and material as this—I have seen it since it was made up into a purse—I saw her working it at her own house, and have seen it since it was finished at her house, and at mine—this is the purse—( this was the purse identified by Mrs. Peppercorn)—I know a mark in the thread as I cut the end of the silk at the time the prisoner was knitting it—here is where it is joined—some remark passed between her and me about it—my brother Andrew came home that night—I accompanied the prisoner home.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. Was it the same evening you made the mark you recognise? A. No, it was completed some time before that.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you see the attorney for the prisoner on the subject of her defence? A. Yes, Mr. Flowers' clerk—she was taken on Friday—I saw him on Sunday evening—I had learned from my sister what passed on the 12th of February, and I went to retain Mr. Flower for the defence.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. You remember this join in the purse? A. Yes, I have had it in my hands repeatedly—when I took it up I expected to find the mark here—it was half done at the time this occurred—it was about where the join is—I was at Bow-street on Tuesday, and took considerable interest in the prisoner's behalf—I was at Bow-street from twelve I believe till two—I was present during the examination o the witnesses—Mr. Flower made a statement to the Magistrate, I believe—I did not—I was present when he made it—I did not state to the Magistrate that I was able to prove the prisoner was not there on the day the
letter was put into the post—my sister was not there—it was no use her going—I believe she was too much affected to go—she was very much hurt—she would have gone if we thought it necessary—no application was made to the Magistrate on the subject—I did not suggest to the Magistrate, if he would listen to the evidence, I could prove she was not there at the time—I did not think it was the proper place.
ANDREW REDGRAVE . I am clerk to Roy, Blunt, and Co., the solicitors to the Mint—I know the prisoner. On the 12th of February, I came home at eight o'clock in the evening, and found my sister, and my brother George, and the prisoner there—she remained at our house till ten that night—she did not leave the house from the time I was there—I know nothing about the purse.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. You are quite sure it was the 12th of February? A. Yes—I have a memorandum of it in my pocket-book, which is here—I know the square housekeeping-book kept in our house—it is a common one, which may be bought at any stationer's shop—it has printed forms in it—I have not taken particular notice of it—I should think it was half as thick as that book—( pointing to one.)
(The prisoner received a good character.)
NOT GUILTY .
(There was another indictment against the prisoner for stealing a letter, containing a purse, on the 8th of October, on which no evidence was offered.)
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420404-1409]
1409. JAMES EDWARDS was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN COOK . I am carman to Messrs. Green and Clark, wharfingers. I have known the prisoner five or six years—I employed him to help me to unload my wagon, and gave him 6 s. a week, and part of his victuals—I paid him with my own money—on the 26th of October, about five or six o'clock in the evening, I told him to go to Mrs. Edwards's, of King's-cross, with five sacks of flour, and to bring back the money, 12 l. 15 s., or the flour—I thought he was a long while gone—at last I went to look after him—he had taken the horse and cart and five sacks of flour with him—I left him with them by Barclay's brewhoase—I saw the hone and cart about ten the same night, in the Borough-market, by itself—I did not we the prisoner again till he was in custody—I then said to him, "Good morning to you, you are quite a stranger"—I did not tell him it would be better for him if he told me any thing, or worse if he did not—he said a harness-maker down the Kent-road had persuaded him to keep the money, and that the harness maker was looking through a beer-shop window at the time—he asked me if I knew the harness-maker—I said I had seen him—I had seen him the same night the prisoner went away—next morning I went to the harness-maker's with a policeman.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did he not say the harness-maker had persuaded him to give him some of the money, and to spend the rest? A. He said he gave him part of the money—I do not think he said any thing about spending the rest—I have been in Messrs. Clark and Green's service three years—the prisoner was known to them before that—he was never employed by them to my knowledge, only by me—I sent him to Mrs. Edwards's with the flour, because I was rather poorly—we were in the Borough-market when I told him to take it, as I had the cart and flour with me—I was to have gone to Mrs. Edwards's myself, but I went home
—I had a pot of beer with the prisoner, and gave him 6 d.—the beer did not affect me—I had taken nothing else—I had not been well for a month or six weeks—if he had received the money and paid it to Clark and Green that would have been sufficient, but I was his master, and I have to give it to them—I gave the prisoner a delivery-note to give to Mrs. Edwards—I received that delivery-note from Clark and Green—I told them the prisoner had not given me the money.
COURT. Q. Did Clark and Green look to you for the money? A. Yes, and I have had to pay them 5 s. a week ever since, to make the money good.
MARGARET EDWARDS . I am the wife of James Edwards, a baker, in Vernon's-buildings, St. Pancras. On the 26th of October, some flour was brought to me by a young man—I cannot say it was the prisoner—it was a much younger person than generally came—I had never seen the person before—he looks better than he did then, if he is the person—he brought me a note with the flour—it was written on the back that he was to take the money back with him, and I paid him 12 l. 15 s.—he wrote on the note, "Received 12 l. 15 s., John Cook"—I put it on my file, afterwards took it to Union-hall, and have since lost it—I have looked for it every where.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you recollect whether the name of Green and Clark was on the note? A. Yes, it was—I have plenty of their notes—I only saw one person—I did not notice any one else come in.
JAMES CUDDY . I am a policeman. On Wednesday morning, the 2nd of February, I was on duty in the London-road—the prisoner came up to me and said he wanted to give himself up—I said, "What for?"—he said for stealing 12 l. 15 s., that he was intrusted by Cook to deliver fire sacks of flour at a baker's shop over against King's-cross, Battle-bridge, that he had received 12 l. 15 s., and absconded, that he turned the horse and cart adrift—he said he was induced to do it by some harness-maker of the name of Hand fast, I believe, that he had given him a part of the money, and had spent the remainder—I said, "Recollect what you tell me, I shall have to state against you before the Magistrate"—he said "I have only told you the truth; you will find it is the truth; I have been to Bristol; I have done no work since; I am in a state of starvation; I I might as well be transported as be out of work, starving about the streets."
Cross-examined. Q. You recollect his saying he had spent some of the money? A. Yes—he said the harness-maker induced him to keep the money, and he had given 5 l. or 6 l. to the harness-maker—I did not take particular notice of the word "spent"—I will not be certain that he used the word "absconded," but he said he went away, and had spent all the money except what he had given the harness-maker.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1410. WILLIAM LISCOMB was indicted for stealing on the 8th of March, 1 quadrant, value 1 l.; 5 shirts, value 10 s.; 4 pairs of stockings, value 2 s.; 3 pairs of trowsers, value 7 s.; 3 waistcoats, value 12 s; 1 coat Value 10 s.; 2 pairs of drawers, value 8 s.; 3 flannel waistcoats, value 12 s; 2 handkerchiefs, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 pair of boots, value 15 s.; and 2 collars, value 1 s; the goods of William Ponfold, his master.
WILLIAM PONFOLD . I am master of the Minx schooner, lying in the Green Dock, Rotherhithe on the 8th of March, I shipped the prisoner on the 3rd, at the wharf—I left him in care of the ship on the 4th of March—I returned on the 8th to get my clothes, and missed my quadrant—I taxed the prisoner with it—he said he knew nothing about it—I over-hauled my clothes, and missed the articles stated—I went to the Thames-police, and on the 9th, I went on board again—the prisoner was then gone—I found him on the 15th—he had my waistcoat and shirt on, which I had missed—these now produced are mine—some have my name on them in full—they were all in the ship when I left him in charge—I have not found the quadrant.
JOHN EASTERBROOK . I am shopman to Mr. Savage, a pawnbroker. I have produced a shirt pawned by the prisoner, on the 8th of March, for 1 s. 6 d.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the waistcoat, shirt, and pair of stockings of a youngster who left the ship; I know nothing of the other things.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Nine Months.
