1411. JOHN COLEMAN and WILLIAM WATSON were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 20lbs. weight of lead, value 5 s., the goods of George Bainbridge, being fixed to a certain building.—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.
COLEMAN pleaded GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.
JOHN STROTHER . I am foreman to George Bainbridge, a timber-merchant, at Rotherhithe—he has an engine-house—on the morning of the 1st of April, I observed the lead was stripped off the hips of the building, and some from the sky-light—I went to a police-station, and got some officers—between our own and another yard we found this lead concealed—it is Mr. Bainbridge's—I set two watchmen to watch.
EZEKIEL HENRY GILBERT . I watched the Ten foot way, where the lead was—I saw the two prisoners coming down a street in company—they went up the Ten foot way direct to the spot where the lead was placed—I saw them look round to see if the coast was clear—Coleman went round the corner of the Ten foot way on to the stone wharf—I went a little farther up the street to see if I could see him remove the lead, and the next I saw of him was with the lead placed under his arm—Watson was then standing in the Ten foot way, about six or eight feet from the lead, apparently on the watch—I called for the key of the stone-wharf, and on approaching Coleman I saw him throw the lead down, close to a shed under some paling—I took Coleman, and gave him in charge of a policeman, went back to the spot where I saw him throw the lead, and there I found it—when Watson was taken he said he knew nothing about it.
GEORGE REDOROVE . I am a policeman. I watched, and saw Watson at five o'clock in the evening in the Ten foot way—I saw Coleman on the premises where the lead was flung—as soon as Gilbert told me had token Coleman I took Watson—I did not see Coleman with the lead.
Watson's Defence. I know nothing about it, neither did I know when
Coleman was there what he was about; I met him against Silver-street as I was going to meet my father; I am a cooper by trade.
WATSON NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420404-1412]
1412. MARY FREEMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 1 crown, 11 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of William Ginn, from his person.
WILLIAM GINN . I live at Frogmore, near Wandsworth, Surrey. Between nine and ten o'clock on the 29th of March I was walking along High-street, Wandsworth, and met the prisoner—she said, "Holloa, old boy, how are you?"—she gave me a shove, and pushed me up against the wall, and began feeling me about—I said to her, " Go along, you hussy,
I don't want any thing to do with you," I parted from her all in a moment, and found my trowsers were undone—I put my hand into my pocket, and 16 s. 6 d. was gone out of a little bag which I kept it in—the bag was left, but it was open—the money could be easily taken out—I had before this taken out a sixpence, and changed it at Wandsworth—I went up the town again, and met the prisoner on the other side of the way—I said, "You hussy, you have robbed me of my money"—she said,
"Here," and returned 7 s. 6 d.—I said, "You have got a crown-piece and some shillings; give me back my crown-piece, I don't want to get into any bother, I have a wife and family at home"—she began abusing me "to all eternity," and said, "Give me in charge of the police"—up came four policemen—I was wrangling with her about the crown-piece—I was going off, thinking it was better to lose the money altogether, as there would only be hell upon earth at home with my wife—I went down the town—the prisoner followed me, and tore my shirt nearly asunder, and began abusing me—the policemen took her, and asked what they should find on her—I said, "Four shillings and a crown-piece"—she was searched, and a crown-piece and four shillings found on her, which was the money I lost—the crown was greasy with putting my hand into my pocket, being in the soap line.
Prisoner. Q. When you met me did you not ask me to go up Chapel-lane with you, and did not you * * * * against my consent? A. No—I was as sober as I am now—I did not know you before—I was not aware you were married.
WILLIAM JENNINGS . I am a policeman. I heard a wrangling on the 29th of March, and saw the prisoner with the prosecutor in High-street, Wandsworth—the prosecutor said to her, "You have robbed me of 16 s. 6 d."—I did not hear what she said—he said, "You have only returned 7 s. 6 d., I don't want to get into trouble with you; return me the 5 s., and I will say no more about it"—with that she assaulted him—she did not say any thing to that—( looking at his deposition)—this is what I said before the Magistrate—it is true—( read)—"she denied it"—the prosecutor said, I will let you go, and say no more about it if you will return me the money.
SARAH GARDENER . I am the wife of Thomas Gardener. I searched the prisoner at the station, and found on her four shillings and a crown-piece—the crown-piece was greasy.
Prisoners Defence. The prosecutor gave me the money; I have a poor husband, who has lost the use of his limbs, and one child; I did no steal the money, nor give any back.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420404-1413]
1413. ANN PRITCHARD was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of March, 1 gown, value 6 s., the goods of John Spragg and another.
WILLIAM JAMES TAYLOR . I am shopman to Mr. John Spragg and Richard Parry, pawnbrokers, at Rotherhithe. I hung this gown on the door within the door-post on the 7th of March—I saw it safe between one and two o'clock—the prisoner came into our shop between three and four—the gown produced is ours—I did not see a woman named Hilbert there that day—she might have been there.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What had the prisoner come to pledge? A. A piece of cotton for 4 d.—I am quite sure she was there that day—I have known her for some years—she has always borne a good character—I missed the gown the next morning.
JOSEPH NEWMAN PERKINS . I am shopmnn to Mr. Perkins, a pawn-broker, at Rotherhithe. I produce the gown pledged in the afternoon by a person named Hilbert—the prisoner was with her.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was the prisoner at the time? A. In the shop—it frequently happens that two persons come together to pledge.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420404-1414]
1414. RICHARD DUST , ANN THORNTON , and MARY FRASER , were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of March, 3 half-sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 5 shillings, the monies of John Monks, from his person.
JOHN MONKS . I am a velvet-weaver, and live in Straits-mouth, Greenwich. On Friday, the 4th of March, about ten o'clock in the evening, I was in a public-house near London-bridge—I had four half-sovereings in my pocket—I changed one, and put the change, which was two half-crowns and five shillings, with the other three half-sovereigns in my right-hand trowsers pocket, expecting to have to pay a few shillings on my road to Deptford—I was going by the railway but it had just closed—I spoke to the prisoner Dust, who is a cab mau, or he to me, to take me—he asked me how far I was going—I said, to Greenwich, that I was going over the way, and if he would come with me I would speak to him—he followed me to the public-house, and I agreed with him to carry me to Deptford-cross—he went out, came in again, and asked if I had any objection to allow a young woman to ride part of the way with me—I said, "Why if it is any benefit to you I have no objection"—he went in and out several times, at last the cab was ready—I got in and was very glad to find no one in it—he drove some little distance, and by a turning as I thought by St. George's church, he pulled up all at once, in the dark and took in two females—I consider it was by St. George's church, but it was dark, and I could not be on my oath as to the exact spot—St George's church is in the road, but he turned down into Kent-street—I had two coats on—I clapped them over so that nobody could get at my pocket—the women came in, sat one on each side of me, and put their hands about me—I put their bands off as well as I could, and said, "You are mistaken, I am an old man, when I was a young man-of-war's-man I might joke with you"—they forcibly dragged my clothes open—one of them shoved her hand right into my trowsers, took hold of me in an indecent manner, and pulled me so hard that I was in pain, and to get her hand away I was obliged to turn to her, and while I did so the other one took every thing I had clean out of
my pocket—I buttoned up as quick as I could, remained still, and took no further notice—I did not find out that my money was gone till I got out at New-cross—I then immediately put my hand into my trowsers' pocket and walked into Seabrook's public-house—my pickets were very deep, I could hardly get my hand to the bottom myself—I found all my money gone from that pocket—I had a trifle in my waistcoat-pocket—I got out of the cab first—immediately on missing my money I said to the cab-man, "I have lost three half-sovereigns"—I do not know exactly what reply he made, but he wanted to run off with his cab —he did not drive off—he was not on the cab but on the ground—he attempted to get on the box—the women were then out of the cab —one of them jumped in again, got out at the other door, and escaped—the other one was standing to go —the cab-man did not say a word to the women when he first of all let them into the cab, nor they to him—they got in immediately he stopped—they shoved in, I could hardly see what they were, and should not have known them again—he helped them in, and shoved them in—I heard no agreement made where they were going, what they were to pay, or anything of the kind.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. I thought you were a velvet-weaver? A. I consider I am—I am also a Greenwich pensioner, and support three orphan children, besides my wife and family—I had come up to London on business that day—I had had three half-pints of beer in the morning, and 4 d, of rum and water in the afternoon—that was all I had drank until this occurred—I was not ten minutes in the public-house by London-bridge—I did not sit down there—I called for a pint of beer, as a shipmate asked me to have a glass of ale with him, but I gave it away to anybody that would drink it—I do not know the exact place where the women got into the cab —I swear I was sober at the time, and collected in every respect.
Q. When the women behaved so indecently, why not call out to the cabman to stop? A. I knew it was no use, for they might have got out and taken away the money altogether—I thought it better to let them be till I caught sight of somebody else—I did not know for certain that I had been robbed till I got out of the cab at the Rose public-house, New-cross—I went into the Rose, but was not there a minute scarcely—if the women went in I never asked them, and knew nothing about it.
Q. Did not you all four go into the Rose public-house after getting out of the cab? A. Like enough—they were no company of mine—I do not know whether they were inside or outside the door—I was not positive I had lost my money till I got into the house—I did not give any money to either of the women in the cab —I am certain of that—I had never seen them before they got into the cab —I might have passed them on the road and not have known them—I never had anything to say or do with them—they might have been in the same public-house where I was, but I never saw them to my knowledge—I have been married thirty-two years, and was married at Hackney church—my wife was in the Court at Greenwich when the examination was going on—I did not say then that I did not like to give any particulars about what passed with the women in the cab, because my wife was there—I said nothing like it—I told the Magistrate that they behaved very indecently—I did not mind my wife hearing it.
Dust. When you came up to the railroad I said I would take you as cheap as anybody; you asked me what I would take you to the Brick-layers
Arms for; I said, "Eighteen-pence;" you said, "I will give you 2 s. to take me to the Post Boy;" I said I would take you; you said, "Come over the way, and I will give you something to drink, I have A friend there;" I went, and saw you at the bar, drinking a pot of beer with a sailor, these women were there in your company, and the waterman is here to prove it; they came out along with you, and were going to get into the cab; the waterman said, "Oh, no;" you said, " oh, let them come in, they are friends of mine" and I was on my box at the time. Witness. The women were not in my company—I did not see them in the house to my knowledge—I said nothing to them whatever.
Dust. When we got to the Post Boy, you told me to go on further; we went on to New Cross; you asked me what the fare was—I said, "Two-and-fourpence, but I hope you will give me half-a-crown;" yon said, "Come in here, and I will pay you;" we went inside; you called for id. worth of rum and water, and gave me half-a-crown; you kept feeling your pockets, and said, "I think I have lost my money"—I said, "If you have, you had better make it right before I go away." Witness. You might have said so, but not as I recollect—I paid you your fare, and 2 d. over—I said, "I am robbed," and followed you to the door—you were outside the door—you did not offer to run away when the policeman came.
Fraser. Q. Were we not at the public-house, by London-bridge, eating bread and cheese when you came in. A. I do not know anything about you—I did not give the cabman 1 s on the road, to pay for a pint of rum—I did not say that two was company, and three none, and say I would give you a trifle if you got out of the cab —I bought a set of razors at the foot of London-bridge, and gate 2 s. for them—the man did not show me a piece of cloth for velvet, nor did I say I would not wear such a paltry thing, that I was a velvet-weaver, and could always cabbage enough.
COURT. Q. How could the woman know all this if she was not with you? A. They might be standing about the Railway Tavern, so they tell me—I did not tell them anything in the cab about my buying razors—I bought the razors of a man who was standing there.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Did any man offer you any cloth for sale? A. No; it is all a fiction—I certainly bought the razors, and paid for them—I did not tell the prisoners I was a velvet-weaver, nor mention it at the tavern—I said it at the office—no woman was going to get into the cab with me at the tavern—I rode off by myself—the cabman had asked me to let a woman ride with me, but I expected he had forgotten it, and I was very glad of it—I did not say, "Let the woman come in"—I never saw the women then.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) I saw the three prisoners and the prosecutor come out of the Rose public-house at New Cross, which is about a mile on this side of Deptford—I heard the prosecutor say, "I have lost my money"—I was on the opposite side of the way—on hearing that I crossed over, and before I could get over I saw the two female prisoners get in at one door of the cab and out at the other, ready to make their escape—I caught Thornton, and called out to my brother constable to bring Fraser back again—we brought them back, and stood them together—I said, "Do you know anything of this man's money?"—they both said they knew nothing of it, they had never seen his money, and all they had
got about them was one silver sixpence; they were willing to be searched there and then—I said, "You must all of you go with me to the station"—at the time the female prisoners got into the cab and out again, Dust was standing at the cab door which they got in at—I said "You must drive to the station"—he said, "Who is going to pay me?"—I said, "Never mind about paying; you will be paid, no doubt"—the prosecutor was perfectly sober.
Fraser. Q. Did not I tell you, going along, that I had a half-crown tied up in the corner of my apron? and did not you say if I offered to touch it you would knock me on the knuckles? A. You might have said so; I did not hear you—when I first took you you said you had not got a halfpenny about you but a silver sixpence—I knew, if you had the money, you would soon make away with it.
MARY WALKER . I am a searcher at the station. I searched the two female prisoners—I found three half-sovereigns and sixpence in Thornton's boot—I found no money on Fraser.
Cross-examined. Q. Was anybody present besides you? A. No—I am quite sure I found the half-sovereigns in Thornton's boot—I do not know which boot it was—she undid them herself—she said it was her husband's money, that he was a chair-maker, in Cheshire-street, Bethnal-green, and he had given her the money that day.
WILLIAM THORN . I am a policeman. I took Fraser—she was running away towards London as fast as she could run—I told her to come back, that she was charged with stealing the money from this old gentleman—she said she had not a farthing of money about her—when we got to the station she gave me half-a-crown and a halfpenny—11 s. 3 d was found on Dust—the prosecutor was perfectly sober.
Dust's Defence. I am a carpenter and undertaker by trade. I have not driven a cab above a month. I tried to get into the police once, and but for a cut on my forehead I should have been admitted. The cab belongs to the Eastern Counties Railway, and I had earned the money found on me that day. I do not ply in the street, but this being after time I did so. I have a witness to prove that the females and the prosecutor were together in the public-house, and that they got into the cab with him.
Fraser's Defence. We were in the public-house by London-bridge about ten minutes to ten o'clock on Friday evening. I had 4 s.; we went and had some bread and cheese, a pint of beer, and a halfpenny orange; the prosecutor and another man was there; he called for a pot of beer, and asked Thornton to drink; she gave me some; he said, "Have you a mind for a ride?" she said to me, "Will you go, Mary?" I said, "I don't mind;" he said, "Don't go in there, there is a friend of mine there." We got into the cab; we had not been in five minutes before he said, "I did not know that two of you were coming, two is company, and three none." I got out and rode with the cabman; when we got a little way he knocked at the window, and asked the cabman to get something to drink, and to get what he liked for himself; he went and fetched half-a-pint of rum, and when we got a little further we had a quartern of gin; when we got to New Cross he pulled out 7 s., and said he wanted some rum and water; he asked me if I had got a penny. I said no, I had only got a half-a-crown and a halfpenny, which I had to take home with me, and Thornton knows the same.
GEORGE HARPER . I am a waterman at the coach-stand at the Brighton
and Greenwich Railway terminus. On Friday evening, the 10th of March, about five minutes past ten o'clock, the prosecutor came up to the railway to go down by the train, but he was late, and the gates were shut—Dust was on the stand—he asked the prosecutor where he wanted to go, that he would take him as cheap as anybody—they made an agreement, and the prosecutor said, "Come over to the public-house, and we will have something to drink, I have got a friend or two there"—they went in, and I stopped by the cab-door —in about a quarter of an hour I saw the women come out of the public-house with the prosecutor—one of them wanted to get into the cab —I said, "No, no, you had better not"—the prosecutor said, "Let her come in, she is a friend of mine"—I would not let her in, but kept my hand on the door—I did not think them proper persons to get in with him—he was intoxicated—I never saw any of them before.
Q. How should you know they did not belong to him? A. Why, I am a pretty good judge of that sort—I am an old hackney-coachman, and have seen a good many transactions—I expected they were women of the town—I swear I saw them come out of the public-house with him—I think it was Fraser wanted to get into the cab —the women went away, and I asked the prosecutor if he had got a penny for me.
(Thornton received a good character.)
THORNTON- GUILTY . Aged 30.
FRASER- GUILTY . Aged 25.
✗ Confined One Year.
DUST— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420404-1415]
1415. CHARLES GRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of February, 1 pencil-case, value 4 s., the goods of Jabez Newsome.
JABEZ NEWSOME . I am a greengrocer, and live in Dover-place West, Kent-road. The prisoner occasionally lodged with me—sometime ago a customer of mine left a pencil-case in my shop, which I put on my sitting-room mantel-piece—I missed it—I said nothing to the prisoner about it—I never gave it to him, or authorized him to take it—this now produced is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Whose pencil-case was this? A. It belonged to a gentleman who left it at my shop—I do not know his name—I have some idea who he is, but am not positive—I think it is Mr. John Hearn's, who came to me on business—he is the only person I can suspect of having brought it—several others had been in the shop the same evening—he lives at No. 19, Trinity-street—I did not write to ask him whether he had left it—I did not think of that—I thought the next time he called I might show it to him—I missed it on the 11th of February, about three days after I had found it, which was about three months ago—as it was missing, it was no use applying to Mr. Hearn—it was found in February—I never sent to him, because it was missing, and if I had it not to produce how could I ask him if it was his?—he has called at my house many times since—I did not tell him I had found a pencil-case, which I thought was his, because it was missing, and I could not produce it—I have never told him up to this moment—I gave the prisoner into custody last Thursday night—I have not seen Mr. Hearn since the pencil-case has been recovered—a letter would reach him certainly—I gave the prisoner in charge first, on suspicion of stealing a coat and umbrella—the coat was the first charge—the coat alone, without the umbrella—he was discharged
by the inspector at the station on that charge, without being taken before a Magistrate—that was on Thursday night—some duplicates were produced on that occasion—I do not know how many—I heard some of them read out at the station—there was nothing to prevent my hearing them all read, but I did not pay attention at the time—I could not, because the prisoner was begging and praying of me at the time to withdraw the charge—the inspector did not read them out very loud—I did not hear the duplicate for this pencil-case read out that night—I gave the prisoner into custody again, about six o'clock next rooming.
Q. Did you, between the Thursday night when he was discharged and six o'clock next morning, pay a visit to his father? A. I did, at five in the morning—I went to tell him I intended to give the prisoner a second time into custody, and that he pleaded very much for me not to proceed with the case, and said he should very likely be transported, or lose his situation—the inspector and the policeman heard the prisoner say that to me in the station—after that the prisoner greatly insulted me—I told his father that, and that there were a great many duplicates in his possession, that I could find a clue, not only to the coat, but also to other things I had missed, and that I had given him in charge again—I had not done so then—that was all that occurred with the father—I swear that—I did not ask him to give me a bill for 10 l. not then—the father called me aside at Union Hall that day after I had given the prisoner into custody a second time—the charge was then for stealing the coat and umbrella—the same coat with which I had charged him the night before—I had consented to withdraw the charge the night before, thinking I might find it by going round to the pawnbrokers—I withdrew it for the present.
COURT. Q. Did the same inspector take the second charge after having discharged him before for the same article? A. Yes—I do not know his name.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. And the same constable took him up, I suppose? A. Yes—he was then taken before a Magistrate—he was not about to be discharged from that when I preferred the present charge—the Magistrate was not about to discharge him, that I know of—I swear that.
Q. If he was not about to be discharged from stealing the coat and umbrella, why have you not indicted him for it? A. The charge was made for the pencil-case, after going round to the pawnbrokers to see if the umbrella belonged to me—he was not indicted for the coat and umbrella, because they were not clearly proved—I saw the duplicate of a pencil-case—I thought it to be mine—I went to the pawnbroker's and examined it, and claimed it, at least as the one that was left at my house—it was not when the Magistrate was about to dismiss the charge about the coat and umbrella, that I preferred this charge—I swear that—I produced the pawnbroker's man to prove that the prisoner had pawned it—the pawn-broker said it was the pencil-case that was brought, but he could not swear to the prisoner as the person that pawned it.
Q. Did not the prisoner upon that, immediately say, "I have nothing to deny, it was me that pawned it—that man (meaning yourself) sold it to me?" A. He acknowledged pawning it—he did so voluntarily, and he immediately said to me, "You stole it"—he said he bought it of me for 3 s 6 d.—the only reason for my not mentioning the pencil-case to Mr. Hearn, was, because it was lost, and I could not then show it him—I do not know why I have not brought him here to identify it—I asked the
prisoner's father to accept a bill for 10l.—the prisoner owed me 19 l. 17 s. 6 d. for lodging—his father called me on one side at Union-hall, and said something to me about giving me his security for the amount his son owed—I directly said, "Mr. Gray, you have partly increased the debt owing by your son, by giving me encouragement to trust him, will you be security for half the amount your son owes me"—he said, "I won't do it"—I had his son in custody at that time—he said, "Supposing I pay you the amount my son owes you"—I made no reply, I only said, "Well," meaning for him to speak on—I do not know what I expected him to say—it was after that I asked him to accept the 10 l. bill—he refused to do so—the prisoner is a clerk in the office of the British Plate Glass Company, at the foot of Black friars-bridge—he had lodged with me about a year and four months.
CHRISTOPHER THOMAS GREAVES . I am a pawnbroker's assistant. This pencil-case was pawned at our house for 2 s. on the 11th of February, in the name of John Wilson—I cannot say who by.
Cross-examined. Q. You were at the Police-office? A. Yes—I have no recollection of the prisoner saying he was the person that pawned it—I heard him say he had bought it of Newsome.
THOMAS TOWNSEND ( police-constable M 223.) I took the prisoner into custody on a charge of stealing a coat and umbrella—that charge was dismissed—he was charged again at six o'clock next morning, on suspicion that it might lead to some other missing property, which had been pledged—the property was not named at that time—no property was named in the second charge—he had a quantity of duplicates in his possession, among which was one for a pencil-case, pledged for 2 s.—that was not named at the time I took him.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been a policeman? A. Eighteen months—the first charge was the coat and umbrella—I am sure the umbrella was named in the first charge—I cannot be mistaken in that—he was set at liberty from that by the inspector—Mr. Newsome gave him in charge the second time, at six o'clock next morning, on suspicion of stealing some more property, not named—he was given in charge on suspicion of felony—that was what Mr. Newsome said—I did not ask what felony—he said he would tell the inspector when he got to the station—I took him to the station—I did not then know what he was charged with—I am positive the umbrella was named in the charge on Thursday night—it was not from the addition of the umbrella to the coat, that he was taken on Friday morning—the inspector is not here—he was only the acting inspector on duty—it was Sergeant Logan—he read over the duplicates in the station on Thursday night—I looked at them, and saw them in his hand—I did not hear him read them out, not to take any notice—I counted them over, and there was thirteen, after the inspector gave them into my hand—I did not read them—he read them out loud—he read them to satisfy himself—he read them over to Mr. Newsome, I believe, and among them was the coat and umbrella—I believe he read them over to Mr. Newsome that he might attend, and claim anything named in them, which belonged to him—I did not pay particular attention to them—I cannot say that Mr. Newsome heard every one more than I did—Mr. Newsome and I were standing outside, and the inspector was inside—I cannot say that he read them to Mr. Newsome.
Q. Did not you say this instant that he read them over to Mr. Newsome? A. And me as well—he read them over certainly, and to Mr.
Newsome—that was on the Thursday night—Mr. Newsome first made the charge about the pencil-case when I took the prisoner to the station the second time—among the duplicates was one for a pencil-case, which Mr. Newsome supposed might be his property, and we went to the pawn-brokers and found it—I believe that duplicate had remained among the rest all night—I have no doubt of it—I was at the office when the pawn-broker's assistant was examined—he could not swear to the prisoner—the prisoner said, "There shall be no difficulty in the case, I pawned the pencil-case," and he said in Mr. Newsome's presence that he had bought it of him for 3 s. 6 d.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420404-1416]
1416. JOHN FINN was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of February, 2 gowns, value 30 s., 1 shawl, value 10 s., and 1 brooch, value 2 s., the goods of Mary Ann Fosdike; and that he had been before convicted of felony: and MICHAEL FINN , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
MARY ANN FOSDIKE . I was on a visit at the house of Mr. Everall, at the Duke on Horseback public-house, in Holland-street—he is not related to me—I had a shawl, two gowns, and a brooch there—these now produced are them—one gown is Orleans cloth—I gave 30 s. for it—I had not worn it a great deal—the other gown I have had some time—it is worth about half-a-crown—the shawl I gave 30 s. for, last Christmas twelvemonth—the brooch was given me by a gentleman—it is Mosaic about ten o'clock on the morning of Wednesday, the 23rd of February, John Finn and a lad named Davis came to the house—they brought a piece of beef-steak and a piece of bread with them—they asked for permission to dress the beef-steak, which I gave them—they remained in the house about an hour and a half, and left about eleven o'clock—my clothes were in my bedroom, up two flight of stairs—I went up stairs to clean myself at twenty minutes past eleven o'clock, and missed my clothes there had been no one in the house but these two lads—there was no Jew named Slipey, or any other person there—I had some conversation with Davis while he was there—he asked me whether I knew him, and told me who he was—that was in presence of John Finn—the next morning I saw Davis at his mothers—he and his mother and father went with me to the house of James Wright, a dealer in marine stores, in Red Cross-street—there was a great many second-hand clothes and things in the shop—in consequence of something said to Wright he produced the clothes to me—Davis's mother paid him some money—I cannot say how much—I saw some silver pass—she paid it for the redemption of the clothes, and the things were delivered to me—I heard Wright say he gave 12 s. 6 d. for them.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Mrs. Davis was a friend of yours, was she not? A No—I had not known her before—I, Davis, and Wright did not arrange that we would let Davis off as easily as possible—I told his mother I would freely forgive him if he would tell me where they were, that I might get them back—the clothes were got back, and on the Tuesday following the prisoners were given into custody—the only person who had any conversation with me was Davis—he did not claim
acquaintance with me—he asked me whether I knew his father—I said no, I did not.
ELIZABETH DAVIS . I am the wife of John Davis, and the mother of the boy. On Wednesday, the 23rd of February, the prosecutrix came to me and told me what she had lost—in consequence of what she said to me I spoke to my son, in consequence of which he told me where the property was—I agreed with the prosecutrix, and went with her and my husband and son to Wright—when we got there these articles were produced—I paid 12 s. 6 d. for them to Wright—the prosecutrix had promised to forgive my son if she got the property.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe your son told you he was a party to the stealing? A. Yes, distinctly.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Who did he tell you was the other party to the stealing? A. John Finn—he said he took the bundle from John Finn.
JAMES WRIGHT . I am a clothes and bottle-merchant, in Red Cross-street—I know Michael Finn by sight—on Wednesday, the 23rd of February last, he came to my shop alone—I had seen him before in the street, but not to speak to him—he brought these two gowns, shawl, and brooch—he asked 1 l. for them, and asked if I would buy them—I asked him whether they were his property—he said they were—I said it was too much—I examined the goods—I supposed him to be twenty-three or twenty-four years of age—I believed him when he said the goods were his own—I thought he might have bought them to sell again as I do—I did not ask him where he lived—I did not ask him whether he bought them to sell again—I said I would give him 12 s. and no more—he took them away and refused to sell them to me—he came back in about twenty minutes or a quarter of an hour, and said I should have—them for I4 s.—I said the goods were not worth more than 16 s., I would give him 12 s. 6 d. and that was all I would give him; if he did not like it, to take the goods out of the place—he took the money and went away—between eight and nine o'clock the next morning, in came five parties to me, and asked if I had purchased the goods of Davis—I said no, I had never seen him; I bought them of a young man who stated he had had the goods for crockery ware—(Michael Finn had told me he had them for crockery ware)—in consequence of the description given of the goods I produced them—the prosecutrix said, "They were stolen out of my house"—I said I was very sorry for it—I told them what I had given for them—they put the money on the counter, and asked me if I gave that money for them—I said, "Yes, that is the money I gave for them"—they were going out of the shop—I stopped the prosecutrix and said, if I saw the party who sold them to me, should I give him in charge?—she said, "No, we do not wish to prosecute for the sake of Davis"—on the 2nd of March I was in the Hat and Feathers public-house, with a friend—I saw Michael Finn there—I called him outside and said I was sorry he had brought the things to me, for he knew they were stolen—he said, "I did not know they were stolen, they were given to me to sell by a party who said he had had them for crockery ware"—he said nothing about trade.
Cross-examined. Q. You returned these on receiving 12 s. 6 d,? A. Yes, they seemed respectable people—I believed they were stolen then—I did not ask for the 12 s. 6 d.—I did not offer to let them go without.
JOHN BLUNDKLL . I am a City-policeman. I was in Gravel-lane
on Wednesday, the 2nd of March, and saw Michael Finn in conversation with Wright—I took him into custody, and told him what he was charged with—he said he was sorry for his mother, but not sorry for himself.
Cross-examined. Q. What was the exact expression he used? A. He did not care for himself, but was sorry for his mother.
WILLIAM HENRY WOLLEN . I am a policeman. In consequence of information, I went to the prosecutrix on Tuesday, the 1st of March—from what she said to me, I went to the house of Davis's mother, and received charge of Davis—I produce the shawl, two gowns, and brooch.
Cross-examined. Q. You received charge of Davis? A. Yes, he was brought to Newgate as a prisoner, and admitted to bail—a bill was preferred against him, which the grand jury threw out.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you the officer in attendance at the examination of the prisoners? A. Yes, I saw the clerk write down what was said at the time by each of them, and they put their marks to it—these are the examinations—( read.)
"Michael Finn says, 'The prisoner Davis gave me the things to sell; my brother was with him; they did not tell me where they got the things; I sold them for 12 s. 6 d.'" "The prisoner, John Finn, says, 'I did not take the things; it was Slipey, a Jew, who was with us, that had the things; it is false what the prosecutrix says, that there was no other person there; she asked me to get change for 6 d. for a man that was there.'"
JOHN DAVIS . I am sixteen years old. I have known John Finn about twelve months. On Wednesday morning, I met him in William-street, Blackfriars-road—he had some meat and some bread—he did not say any thing to me about it—he bought the meat and bread while I was with him—he asked me if I would go with him to the Duke on Horseback—we took the meat there, cooked it, and we had some beer—after that he took off his shoes in the tap-room, and went up stairs—when he came down he had a bundle—he was going to put it out of window—I said, "Don't put it out of window, I will take it out under my arm"—I did so—I went out first, and waited outside—in about five minutes he came out—I went with him to his house. No. 19, York-street, Black friars-road, and left the bundle there—I never go by the name of Slipey —there was no Jew or any person with us at the Duke on Horseback—I afterwards went with him to the Hat and Feathers public-house, and saw Michael Finn there-John told him he had a bundle, and asked him to go and sell it—he went with us home to his own house, and afterwards we went to Wright's shop, and sold the things for 8 s. 6 d., so Michael Fim told me—he gave me 3 s. 6 d. John Finn had 3 s. 6 d., and Michael 1 s. 6 d.
John Finn. Slipey had the rest. Witness. I do not know slipey.
John Finn. Slipey is the one that went up stairs.
MARY ANN FOSDIKE re-examined. There was no third person there who could be called Slipey there was no one in the tap-room but the two boys.
John Finn. Q. Did not you send me out for change for 6 d., for a man? A. There was a gentleman in front of the bar, who had a glass of ale, but he went away immediately—he did not come in at all—I am sure he was not Slipey
John Finn. I went next door, got change for 6 d., and put the half-pence
on the counter. Witness. I sent him for change—I did not go up stairs till five or ten minutes after the boys were gone.
GEORGE VINCENT . I was a policeman of the M division—I produce a certificate of John Finn's former conviction, in May, 1839—( read)—he is the person—I was present at his trial.
JOHN FINN— GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.—Convict Ship.
MICHAEL FINN- NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18420404-1418]
1418. JAMES SIMMONS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, I watch, value 2 l. 10 s.; 1 watch-chain, value 2 s.; 1 seal, value 1.; and 1 watch-key, value 1 s.; the goods of Joseph Tuckfield, from his person.
JOSEPH TUCKFIELD . I am foreman of the Deptford sawyers. I was going from the City over London-bridge, on the 6th of April, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening—there were a good many people assembled there, looking at some exhibition in the street—I was making my way through the crowd, and four or five men came round me—the prisoner, who was one of them, put his hand round me, and at the same moment I felt my watch go from my pocket—I instantly seized the prisoner by the collar, and told him he had stolen my watch—he said, "Let me go, I have not;" and three or four men said, "Let him go" and tried to extricate him from me—I held him as well as I could—he struggled very hard to get from me—I succeeded in getting him into a beer-shop—Price came to the beer-shop, and took the prisoner to the station—I have not seen my watch and chain again—I cannot say exactly the value of it—it cost me 5 l. two or three years ago.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. About how many persons were there altogether? A. About fifty—it was rather duskish—there were plenty of lights—I had never seen the prisoner before in my life—he struggled and broke from my grasp, but I never lost sight of him for a moment—as he got from me I caught him again—he was searched in the beer-shop, and nothing of mine found on him.
FRANCES KIRBY . I am married, and live in Red Cross-court. I was in Wellington-street on the 6th of April—I do not know what tine it was—I was coming by in the crowd, and saw the prosecutor—he said he had lost his watch—I said it was a shame—there was a man selling pipes in the street—I could not tell what sort of a man he was—I saw the prisoner—I could not swear to him at all—he had a hat on then—I cannot swear to him, because he has no hat on—I saw the person that was laid hold of, but he got away again; and I could not say it was him at all—there were a good many persons, I could not say how many—I cannot tell exactly whether I saw the prisoner—I did not see him do any thing, only getting through the crowd, like a good many more—the prosecutor caught hold of him, and I could not say what the man had done—I saw a man coming by with a watch, I cannot say whether it was the
prisoner or not—I did not see any man take a watch—when the alarm was given, I saw the watch in a man's hand—they said it was the man's, and I said what a shame it was to rob a poor man.
Q. Recollect yourself—did you ever say you saw a person take a watch out of a gentleman's pocket? A. Why if I said so, I said very wrong—I never, as I know of, said that I saw some persons surround the prosecutor—they took me to the police-office, and I had some fish in my lap—I did not say any thing there—I said nothing at all about it—I said I saw a lot of people surround the prosecutor, when he lost his watch.
Q. Did you ever say you saw a man pull his watch out by the chain? A. Why, a man said he had lost his watch—I never said that I saw a person pull his watch out of his pocket, as I recollect.
Q. Did you ever say you pointed out the prisoner, and said, "That is the man? A. I do not recollect the prisoner at all—I was never in such a place as this before.
Cross-examined. Q. There was a great crowd, I believe? A. There was—I cannot say how many people there were there—I had a lot of fish in my lap, which quite confused me.
JOSEPH TUCKFIED re-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. The man who caught hold of me had a black silk waistcoat on, and a black coat—I cannot tell what coloured cravat he had—I cannot say whether he had a cap or a hat on, but I believe a hat; yes, it was a hat—I did not for the moment recollect—I recollect its partly falling off his head, and he caught it again, and put it on—that was when I had hold of him—I did not see either of the witnesses—the girl said in the crowd that she saw the man take the watch from my pocket, and she came forward as a willing witness.
GEORGE WORTHY . I am a paper-stainer, and live in King-street, Bethnal-green. I was in Wellington-street on the evening in question—there was a man selling glass pipes in the street—I saw a person very much like the prisoner there; but since then I have seen so many persons so much like him, that I could not say it was him—I saw a man with his arms round the prosecutor's body—the prosecutor turned round, and said, "I have lost my watch"—he seized hold of a man that stood close by, and accused him of having his watch—I did not exactly see whether the man he laid hold of was the man who had his arms round him—I have said that I saw the prisoner with his arms round the prosecutor—I have never said that I heard Kirby say any thing about the person who took the watch—I gave evidence at Union-hall—I said there that Kirby pointed out the prisoner to the prosecutor, and said, "That is the man that robbed you"—I did not understand what you said before—I saw the prosecutor lay hold of somebody—I cannot say it was the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. About how many persons were there in the crowd? A. There might have been upwards of sixty or seventy—it was duskish—it was a place where a man might very likely make a mistake.
Q. How came you to say that Kirby said that the person the prosecutor had hold of was the man that robbed him? A. I heard her say so at the police-court; no, not at the police-court, at the beer-shop at the foot of London-bridge—she did say so at the police-office—I have said I saw the prisoner with his arms round the prosecutor, but I cannot swear to him now, I have seen so many people like him—the prisoner has been
in prison—I have never seen him since—from what I saw, it appeared that the person who was attempting to grasp the prosecutor did it to protect him. NOT GUILTY .
( The Policeman stated that he had seen the witnesses Kirby and Worthy in company with the prisoner's friends in the street. They were committed for contempt of Court.)
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, MAY 9TH, 1842.
